# HTTP API

The REST JSON API of [share-tracker](../README.md). The tables behind it are documented in [SCHEMA.md](SCHEMA.md).

All data endpoints return JSON. Write endpoints accept `Content-Type: application/json`.

**Base path.** Every path below is written as served at the root, which is the default. When the server is configured with a `base_path` (`--base-path /share_tracker`, for [running behind a reverse proxy](../README.md#behind-a-reverse-proxy) on a sub-path), the whole application — these endpoints and the [web frontend](#web-frontend) alike — moves under that prefix: `GET /listings` becomes `GET /share_tracker/listings`. Nothing else about the API changes, and the unprefixed paths are then not served at all. The bare prefix with a trailing slash (`/share_tracker/`) answers `307` to the prefix itself.

**Authentication.** Every path below is open by default. See [Authentication](#authentication) for the optional `[auth]`-gated form (a session cookie or bearer token required on everything, `401`/`303` otherwise).

## Web frontend

The server also hosts a built-in web UI — a no-build-step single-page app (plain HTML/CSS/JS, shipped as native ES modules) embedded in the binary and served from the same origin as the API:

| Method | Path | Description |
|--------|------|-------------|
| `GET` | `/` | The SPA shell (HTML) |
| `GET` | `/static/app.js` | The app entry module: the rendering engine and router (JavaScript) |
| `GET` | `/static/config.js` | The entity/report/action configuration the engine renders, incl. the top menu bar's `MENUS`/`menu`/`section` fields (JavaScript) |
| `GET` | `/static/nav.js` | The top menu bar: its config-driven model and rendering (JavaScript) |
| `GET` | `/static/chart.js` | The inline-SVG time-series graph and its date-range helpers (JavaScript) |
| `GET` | `/static/forms.js` | Field constructors and form wiring (JavaScript) |
| `GET` | `/static/taxreport.js` | The Annual Tax Report's bespoke print-document renderer (JavaScript) |
| `GET` | `/static/util.js` | Shared utilities: API client, formatting, decimal arithmetic (JavaScript) |
| `GET` | `/static/style.css` | Stylesheet (CSS), incl. the Annual Tax Report's `@media print` rules |

Under a configured `base_path` these asset routes move under the prefix with everything else. The frontend learns where it is mounted from a `<meta name="base-path">` tag the server substitutes into the shell, and prefixes every URL it sends to the server through one helper (`apiUrl` in `util.js`) — so the UI is identical whether it is served at `/` or at `/share_tracker/`. Hash routes need no prefixing: the fragment never reaches the server.

Open `http://localhost:<port>/` in a browser — it opens straight on the **Portfolio Overview**, the app's home screen (`#/`; `#/r/overview` is the same view, reached from the Reports menu). Navigation is a menu bar across the top rather than a sidebar: **Activity**, **Reports**, **Reference Data**, and **Jobs**, each expanding a panel of its screens on hover, keyboard focus, or click (so it works for mouse, keyboard, and touch alike); the Reports panel, holding far more screens than the other three, is a mega-menu of titled columns (Portfolio, CGT & tax, Decision support, Cross-checks & alerts) rather than one long list. The current screen's menu is highlighted even with its panel closed. The Portfolio Overview carries shortcut buttons — **New trade**, **New income**, **New sell**, **New transfer** — linking straight to those entry forms, since recording a trade or a distribution is the most common thing done from this screen.

The app is hash-routed (`#/e/<entity>`, `#/sells`, `#/jobs`, `#/attachments/<owner>/<id>`, `#/renames/<listing>`, `#/r/<report>`) and drives the JSON API below — it provides CRUD screens for every entity (exchanges, listings, holding accounts, trades, income, investment expenses, AMMA statements, AMIT adjustments, DRP enrolments, exchange holidays, CGT settings, corporate actions), a dedicated Sell screen that captures parcel allocations atomically, a Transfers screen that moves parcels between holding accounts (`PUT /transfers/:id`) and deletes a transfer to restore the pre-transfer holding, a simple-first income form (payment amount + franking selector mapped onto the component fields, the per-share cross-check pair with a live computed-product hint, and a "Reinvested under DRP" tick that chains the reinvest call after the save, with the full tax-component field set behind an advanced toggle), a **Rename** action on listing rows (`POST /listings/:id/rename` — the dated, audited ticker or exchange change a `PUT` refuses on a listing with recorded trades, income, or prices, so the endpoint that refusal names is reachable from the row it is raised on; see [Ticker or name changes](#listings)) beside a **Rename history** view (`#/renames/<listing>`) listing that listing's recorded chain newest-first from `GET /listings/:id/renames`, with an **Undo** on the newest entry only (`DELETE /listings/:id/renames/:rename_id` — the chain unwinds last-in-first-out and the API refuses any other, so it is offered nowhere else), a DRP reinvest action on income rows (with an Undo reinvest action on reinvested rows driving `DELETE /income/:id/reinvest`), an Exercise and a Sell rights action on RightsIssue corporate-action rows (`POST /corporate_actions/:id/exercise` / `POST /corporate_actions/:id/sell_rights` — the latter with an anchoring-parcel allocation editor; recorded sales are listed under a delete-only Rights Sales view whose Delete undoes the sale and frees the entitlement), a Participate action on BuyBack corporate-action rows (`POST /corporate_actions/:id/participate`), an Exchange action on ScripForScrip corporate-action rows (`POST /corporate_actions/:id/exchange`), a Demerge action on Demerger corporate-action rows (`POST /corporate_actions/:id/demerge`), a Recognise action on WorthlessShares corporate-action rows (`POST /corporate_actions/:id/recognise`), an ESS Statements screen with a Vest action on unvested rows that creates the cost-base-reset Buy for vested shares (`POST /ess_statements/:id/vest`; a vested row shows its linked Buy instead), an Attachments action on each trade (including Sells)/income/AMMA/ESS-statement/interest-income/corporate-action row that uploads, lists, downloads, and deletes its documents (a trade created from another record — a DRP trade, an ESS vest Buy, a buy-back Sell — also lists that record's [linked documents](#attachments), labelled with their owner; delete stays on the owner's view), read-only views of the import-managed reference tables (currencies, MIC registry, RBA FX rates, parcel allocations), a Jobs menu that lists the scheduled jobs with each one's last run (when it finished, whether it succeeded, and any error), expands each job to its stored run history (the newest 20 runs, so an intermittent failure is diagnosable), and runs any of them on demand (`POST /jobs/:name`), and a view for each report (portfolio overview, open parcels, attachments, unrealised/realised gains, performance, net capital gain, tax summary, exchange MIC validation, FX coverage, settlement holiday coverage). The net capital gain and tax summary report views carry an **Export CSV** action that downloads the report via its `/export` endpoint. An **Annual Tax Report** view (see [Annual tax report](#annual-tax-report)) is a year-picker + Generate button producing a printable, per-year tax document — trading gains/losses with a full itemised cost-base breakdown, the ATO gain/loss worksheet, income by category, and a data-completeness check — rendered as plain semantic tables rather than the shared `filterableTable` (a print document has no business with a filter row, sort indicators, or a pager), with a Print / Save as PDF button (`window.print()`) over a dedicated `@media print` stylesheet. A **Snapshots** view (Jobs menu) lists the stored [report snapshots](#report-snapshots) with stale and provisional ones badged, opens any day's stored rows, generates/regenerates a day on demand, offers a date-ranged **Regenerate all** bulk-repair button (prefilled from `GET /report_snapshots/regenerate_range` to span the first-ever holding through the latest fully-valuable date, so one click can also backfill dates that never had a snapshot) alongside a **Regenerate provisional** button (`POST /report_snapshots/regenerate_all` / `regenerate_provisional`); the market-value graph itself is on the Portfolio Overview, as an inline-SVG time series (no chart library — the no-build-step rule holds) with stale points hollow and provisional points dash-ringed, quick-select date-range presets (1M/3M/6M/1Y/2Y/3Y/FY-to-date/all) whose choice persists across reloads via `localStorage` (a custom From/To range clears the remembered preset instead), and a default-checked "hide holdings with no activity in this period" checkbox (also remembered) over the per-holding contributions table.

**Health banner:** every view carries a cross-page warning strip driven by `GET /reports/health` (see [Health](#health)). It appears only when something needs attention — stale closing prices, stale RBA FX rates, a job whose latest run failed, a listing with errored closing prices, two listings holding one price series between them, a duplicated corporate action, or a listing whose currency is not the one its exchange quotes in — names the problem, and links to the page it is fixed on (Jobs, Closing Prices, Corporate Actions, Listings); it refreshes on each navigation, so fixing the cause (e.g. re-running the failed job) clears it. A failing price or FX import is therefore visible from any screen, not only when the Jobs page happens to be opened.

**Names, never raw ids:** every foreign-key id shown in the UI renders the referenced row's name, not the bare number — in entity-list tables, report tables, `<select>` option labels, the post-record action pages, and the toast that confirms a created row. A listing shows as `MIC:TICKER` (`Crypto:TICKER` for crypto), a holding account by its name, a trade/parcel as a side/quantity/listing/date description (e.g. "DRP 45 XASX:VDHG on 2024-12-20"), and an AMMA statement as its listing + tax year. The raw id stays reachable on the cell's tooltip and appears only as secondary detail (e.g. "Reinvested into DRP 45 XASX:VDHG on 2024-12-20 (trade #12)"). This is display-only — the JSON API is unchanged and still keyed by id.

**Operation provenance (Origin):** the Trades and Sells lists carry a derived **Origin** column labelling any row an operation created rather than a directly entered trade — a transfer leg ("Transfer #3 in"/"out"), a scrip-exchange or demerger rollover Buy, a rights exercise, an ESS vest, an inheritance, a buy-back or worthless-shares Sell. The rollover-style Buys (transfer-in, scrip replacement, demerger) carry the moved parcel's cost base on the `brokerage` column with a zero price, so their label spells out that the brokerage figure is a carried cost base, not a fee (a rights-exercise Buy's notes it is the rights cost). Display-only and derived client-side from the provenance link columns the trade JSON already carries; ordinary trades show an empty Origin.

**Human-friendly headings:** every heading, table column header, and form field label reads as a human name, never the raw database/JSON field name — `amount_per_security` shows as "Amount per security", `fx_rate` as "FX rate", `exchange_mic` as "Exchange", `holding_account_id` as "Account". A field with no explicit label is humanised by default: a trailing `_id` is dropped (the cell already shows the referenced row's name, so `listing_id` → "Listing"), the snake_case becomes sentence case, and known acronyms keep their canonical casing (AUD, FX, MIC, DRP, CGT, AMIT, GST, LIC, FITO) rather than "Aud"/"Fx"/"Drp". The always-AUD report aggregates carry an "(AUD)" qualifier (e.g. "Market value (AUD)"); per-row entity tables get none, because their amounts are in the row's own currency. This is the labelling counterpart to *Names, never raw ids* (that fixed raw id values; this fixes raw field names in the headers around them) and is likewise display-only and config-driven — keyed by column name, so a column reused on a new screen inherits its heading automatically.

**Amounts round, rates don't:** in every table (entity lists, the Sells/Transfers lists, and report tables) a numeric column is classified by name as a monetary amount, a per-unit rate, or a quantity. Monetary amounts are shown rounded to the cent (2 decimal places, half away from zero) with thousands grouping (e.g. `123,476.78`); per-unit rates (a trade's average price, an income amount-per-security, a DRP reinvestment price, FX rates, crypto/closing prices) and quantities keep their full entered precision, because rounding a rate would break statement reconciliation; a derived per-unit figure a report computes (e.g. average cost base per unit) shows at least 4 decimal places rather than cent-rounded. This is display-only and exact (decimal-string arithmetic, never floating point): the JSON API and the CSV exports still return full-precision decimals, and the underlying value drives column sorting (filters match the displayed text). When rounding a money cell drops precision, the full value is shown on its hover tooltip. A new screen inherits the rule automatically — the classification is keyed by column name, shared across the API.

## Authentication

Off by default: unless the server is started with an `[auth]` table in its config file (see [README "Authentication"](../README.md#authentication)), no endpoint below requires anything and this section doesn't apply. When `[auth]` **is** configured, every path in this document — including the [web frontend](#web-frontend) — requires one of:

- a valid `st_session` cookie, obtained via the endpoints below; or
- `Authorization: Bearer <api_token>`, when `[auth].api_token` is configured — for scripts calling the API without a browser (`pkg/freebsd/update.sh`, `smoke-test.sh`).

A request with neither answers `401` (or, for a browser navigation — an `Accept` header naming `text/html` — a `303` to `/login` instead; see [Response codes](#response-codes)). `GET /login`, `POST /login`, and `GET /static/style.css` are always reachable, unauthenticated, so the login page itself can render.

| Method | Path | Description |
|--------|------|-------------|
| `GET` | `/login` | The sign-in page (HTML) |
| `POST` | `/login` | `application/x-www-form-urlencoded` body `username`, `password`. Correct credentials answer `303` to the app root with `Set-Cookie: st_session=...` (`HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax`, and `Secure` unless `[auth].secure_cookie = false`); incorrect credentials re-render the sign-in page with `200` and no cookie |
| `POST` | `/logout` | Answers `303` to the app root with a clearing `Set-Cookie` (`Max-Age=0`). Client-side only — see below |

The session cookie is self-contained (its own signature and expiry, checked against a key derived from the configured password hash) rather than a database-backed session: there is no session table, and nothing here is in [SCHEMA.md](SCHEMA.md) or the [audit trail](#row-history) as a result. Two consequences of that:

- changing `[auth].password_hash` invalidates every previously issued session at once (the derived key changes with it) — the intended way to force everyone signed out;
- `POST /logout` **cannot revoke a specific cookie** — it only tells the browser to stop sending it. A copied-out cookie value stays valid until its own 30-day expiry regardless of logging out. See [Known limitations](#known-limitations).

No CSRF token is issued or required: the cookie's `SameSite=Lax` withholds it from cross-site `POST`/`PUT`/`DELETE` requests, which covers every state-changing route in this API (`GET` routes are read-only).

## Exchanges

| Method | Path | Description |
|--------|------|-------------|
| `GET` | `/exchanges` | List all exchanges |
| `GET` | `/exchanges/:mic` | Get one exchange |
| `PUT` | `/exchanges/:mic` | Create or update an exchange |
| `DELETE` | `/exchanges/:mic` | Delete an exchange |

Seed data includes `XASX` (ASX, T+2) and `XNYS` (NYSE, T+2). `PUT` returns `422` if `currency` is not a recognised code in `currencies`. `close_time` (`HH:MM` local in the exchange's `timezone`, default `16:00`) is the end of the regular trading session: [closing-price collection](#closing-prices) only stores a day's price once it has passed.

`DELETE` returns `422` while anything still references the exchange — its [listings](#listings), its [listing renames](#listings), or its own [holiday calendar](#exchange-holidays) — naming each blocking table and its row count (see [Deletes blocked by a dependant](#deletes-blocked-by-a-dependant)). A seeded exchange's holidays are part of the calendar, so an exchange that has ever been seeded has to have them removed before it can go.

## Exchange holidays

Full-closure non-trading days per exchange, keyed by `(mic, holiday_date)`. Settlement-date calculation skips these in addition to weekends (see [Trades](#trades)). Seeded from the published NYSE and ASX calendars for 2019–2027 (extend as later years are published).

Coverage is finite: an exchange's calendar is considered to cover the whole calendar years spanned by its seeded holidays (1 Jan of the earliest holiday's year to 31 Dec of the latest's). Outside that span, settlement calculation degrades to weekend-only skipping — this is never an error, but it is surfaced rather than silent: the write logs a `WARN` and the [Settlement holiday coverage](#settlement-holiday-coverage) report flags the affected trades until the missing years are entered.

| Method | Path | Description |
|--------|------|-------------|
| `GET` | `/exchange_holidays` | List all holidays (ordered by MIC, then date) |
| `GET` | `/exchange_holidays/:mic` | List one exchange's holidays (ordered by date) |
| `GET` | `/exchange_holidays/:mic/:date` | Get one holiday (`:date` is `YYYY-MM-DD`) |
| `PUT` | `/exchange_holidays/:mic/:date` | Create or update a holiday (body: `{ "name": "..." }`) |
| `DELETE` | `/exchange_holidays/:mic/:date` | Delete a holiday |

`PUT` returns `422` if `:mic` is not a known exchange, and `400` if `:date` is not a valid `YYYY-MM-DD` date.

Every route is keyed on the natural `(mic, holiday_date)` pair, and that is unchanged. Each holiday additionally carries a server-assigned `id` in the responses (migration 0039): it is the row's identity for the [audit trail](#row-history), so a history lookup is `{"table": "exchange_holidays", "row_id": <id>}`. Nothing is ever addressed by it — a `PUT` to the same `(mic, date)` updates the existing row and keeps its `id`, and an `id` sent in a body is ignored.

The calendar is **not** only a settlement input: [valuation](#report-snapshots) reads it live, valuing each holding at its nearest trading day on or before the date being valued. So a `PUT` or `DELETE` here re-values every stored [report snapshot](#report-snapshots) dated on or after the holiday, and marks those snapshots **stale** in the same transaction (migration 0033) — the daily snapshot job regenerates the ones inside its window, and older dates are repaired by `POST /report_snapshots/regenerate_all`. A `PUT` that only corrects an existing holiday's `name` stales nothing: the name is informational and no calculation reads it.

Because the calendar changes a reported figure and is hand-maintained — there is no import to re-derive it from, the published calendars being a one-off seed — it is also an **audited** table (migration 0039, decided 2026-08-21): every `PUT` that replaces an existing holiday and every `DELETE` records the prior row in [row history](#row-history), `name` included. A deleted holiday is recoverable from nothing else, so that is where it survives. The two mechanisms answer different questions and are deliberately not narrowed alike: a name-only `PUT` stales no snapshot (no stored figure moved) but is still recorded (the row did say something else before).

## Listings

| Method | Path | Description |
|--------|------|-------------|
| `GET` | `/listings` | List all listings |
| `GET` | `/listings/:id` | Get one listing |
| `PUT` | `/listings/:id` | Create or update a listing |
| `DELETE` | `/listings/:id` | Delete a listing |
| `POST` | `/listings/:id/rename` | Record a ticker or exchange change as a dated event |
| `GET` | `/listings/:id/renames` | The listing's rename chain, newest first |
| `DELETE` | `/listings/:id/renames/:rename_id` | Undo the newest rename in the chain |

`PUT` returns `422` if `exchange_mic` is not a known exchange or `currency` is not a recognised code in `currencies`. The same currency check applies to the `currency` (and `brokerage_currency`) fields on trades, income, and AMMA writes. `PUT` also returns `422` if it would change `ticker` or `exchange_mic` on a listing that already has any recorded trades, income, or closing prices — see **Ticker or name changes** below — and likewise for a change to `currency`, which is part of the identity for the same reason and one more: every stored [closing price](#closing-prices) is denominated in it, so a change would silently re-value the whole price history (the same stored `200` is A$298.51 as USD and A$333.33 as EUR) while each trade keeps the currency it was recorded in. Unlike a ticker change it has no rename path: **a redenomination is a new listing in the new currency plus a [transfer](#transfers) of the parcels to it**, which keeps every stored price with the currency it was quoted in. The refusal names both currencies. The same rule closes the one door that would otherwise bypass it — `POST /listings/:id/rename` refuses a move to an exchange quoting a different currency, and [Health](#health) names any listing that is already in that state (see **Ticker or name changes** below). `price_symbol` (optional, nullable `TEXT`) overrides the derived provider symbol used to collect [closing prices](#closing-prices) — see there for when to set it.

**A fund that *converted* to an AMIT.** `amit` says a security is an [Attribution Managed Investment Trust](#amma-statements); `amit_from` (optional, nullable, a **1 July** date) says *from when*, for a MIT that elected into the regime part-way through your holding. Every reader of the flag compares its own record's financial year against it — the [income](#income) write-time checks, the [tax summary](#tax-summary)'s whole-row exclusion, the [AMIT cash cross-check](#amit-cash-cross-check), the [annual tax report](#annual-tax-report)'s completeness section *and* its printed income rows, the [franking at-risk](#franking-at-risk) holding-period walk, and the `ReturnOfCapital` refusal — so the pre-conversion years stay **ordinary trust income**: their distributions keep their franking credits, LIC deductions and tax-deferred amounts, their E4 [return-of-capital](#corporate-actions) reductions stay enterable *and* editable, no AMMA statement is expected for them, the tax summary still reports them, the annual tax report still prints the rows behind that report's figures, and their attached franking credits are still holding-period tested and still count toward the year's A$5,000 small-shareholder total. From the dated year on, the AMIT rules apply as usual.

Leave `amit_from` out for a fund that has always been an AMIT — the flag then covers the whole recorded history, which is what every existing listing means. `PUT` returns `422` if `amit_from` is set without `amit` (the date has no status to date), or if it is not a 1 July date: AMIT status is *elected for an income year* (`docs/ato/amit-reporting-requirements.md`), so it turns on at a year boundary — a mid-year date would leave one financial year's income partly attributed and partly assessed as ordinary trust income. Use the 1 July on which the fund's first AMIT financial year began (FY2025 → `2024-07-01`).

**A security the provider has stopped quoting.** `unpriced_from` (optional, nullable date) records the day from which the price provider serves **nothing** for the listing — a delisting, or a suspension that can run for years. Without it, one such holding stops the whole portfolio: every trading day after its last quote stores an errored [closing price](#closing-prices), [valuation](#report-snapshots) refuses the *whole* date if any held listing is unpriced (deliberately — a partly-priced day yields no result at all), so the daily `report-snapshot` job is blocked for **every** listing, every day, while [Health](#health) nags with a growing `errored_days` count. The documented way out — a price entered by hand — is one entry per listing per trading day, indefinitely. From `unpriced_from` on:

- **collection stops fetching the listing.** The scheduled `price-import` job skips those days; `POST /closing_prices/fetch` refuses such a date `422` naming the marker, and `POST /closing_prices/backfill` clamps its range to the day before it (refusing `422` if the whole range is inside the unpriced run). So no *fetched* row can appear on or after the date once it is set.
- **[Health](#health) stops reporting it.** Errored rows and unpriced days dated from it drop out of `errored_prices` / `unpriced_days` — they are expected, not a to-do. Rows *before* the date are still real holes and still reported.
- **valuation carries the last stored ok close forward** instead of blocking the date, and flags the result `price_carried_forward` — on the [snapshot](#report-snapshots), on the report rows, and on [period performance](#period-performance). The substitution is never silent, and it is deliberately **not** the `provisional` flag: that one means an interim FX rate a later import trues up, while a carried-forward price never clears.

A price **entered by hand** on or after the date is still accepted and still wins: valuation prefers a stored ok price for the valuation day itself to the carried-forward one, so an administrator's valuation during a suspension can be recorded normally.

`PUT` returns `422` if `unpriced_from` has no stored closing price **before** it (that earlier close is the figure valuation carries forward, so without one the date stays blocked either way — backfill the history, or enter one price by hand, first), or if a **fetched** ok price is stored **on or after** it (the provider is serving exactly what the column says it does not; the refusal names the offending date). Clearing `unpriced_from` — the security relists, the suspension lifts — marks every [report snapshot](#report-snapshots) dated on or after it **stale**, so they regenerate at the prices collected since instead of keeping a flat line at the last close.

**A security whose provider series has not begun.** `unpriced_before` (optional, nullable date) is the mirror image: it records the day the price provider's series **begins** for the listing, before which nothing is obtainable at any price. This is the shape a spun-off entity has — New Lithium Americas' Yahoo series starts 2023-10-02, and a backfill of any earlier range answers HTTP 400 on every day. A carry-forward cannot reach it: there is no earlier close to carry. Before `unpriced_before`:

- **collection never fetches the listing.** The scheduled `price-import` job skips those days; `POST /closing_prices/fetch` refuses such a date `422` naming the marker, and `POST /closing_prices/backfill` starts its range at the date (refusing `422` if the whole range falls before it).
- **[Health](#health) stops reporting it.** Errored rows and unpriced days dated before it drop out of `errored_prices` / `unpriced_days`, for the same reason they do from `unpriced_from` on.
- **valuation excludes the holding** from that date's portfolio totals instead of blocking the date, and flags the result `holding_excluded` — on the [snapshot](#report-snapshots), whose `excluded_holdings` names each absent holding and why, on the report rows (left unvalued carrying the reason as `price_unavailable`), and on [period performance](#period-performance).

The two directions are deliberately **not symmetric**. Carrying a close forward substitutes a real, once-observed price; nothing before the provider's series begins was ever observed, so **no figure is invented** — the holding leaves the total and the total says so. Two consequences follow, both visible rather than silent: the Portfolio Overview graph **steps** where the listing's own series begins, and a portfolio total for the excluded span **omits a real holding**. `holding_excluded` is its own flag for the reason `price_carried_forward` is not `provisional` (an excluded holding never clears, and the true-up runs select on `provisional`) and for one more: those two say the figure rests on an interim input, this one says the figure is *missing a holding*.

Unlike `unpriced_from`, the marker **supersedes any closing price already stored** for those days, whatever its origin. It is a listing-level declaration made later in time than the rows, and that is what lets a span priced from another security's series be retired rather than quietly continuing to price it. To value a day inside the span from a source you do have, move `unpriced_before` back to the earliest day you can price (and enter that price by hand), or clear it.

`PUT` returns `422` only if `unpriced_before` and `unpriced_from` would leave **no day the provider serves** — `unpriced_before` must fall strictly before `unpriced_from` when both are set. Both set is an ordinary shape: a security quoted only between two dates (a spun-off entity later delisted) is exactly what the pair is for, and the close `unpriced_from` carries forward is then taken from inside that window, never from a row before the series began. There is deliberately **no** rule about prices already stored before the date, in either direction: nothing is carried, so nothing need exist, and *refusing* one would make the feature unreachable for the case it exists for — a fetched row before the date is not proof the provider serves the listing's own symbol, since `POST /closing_prices/backfill` takes a one-off `symbol` override. Clearing `unpriced_before`, or moving it back, marks every [report snapshot](#report-snapshots) dated **before** it stale, so they regenerate with the holding back in the totals.

**Crypto listings:** a crypto asset held as an investment is a CGT asset like any share (`docs/ato/crypto-cgt.md`), recorded as a listing with `security_type: "Crypto"` and **no `exchange_mic`** (omit it or send null — a crypto asset trades on no MIC-coded venue). Its `ticker` must be a recognised digital-token code in [`currencies`](#currencies) (kind `DigitalToken` — the seeded BTC/ETH codes or anything the ISO 24165 import recognises, matched on the DTI code or short name). **Only BTC and ETH are recognised out of the box**: every other token arrives with the [ISO 24165 (DTIF) import](#currencies), which is skipped unless its credentials are configured, so the refusal for an unrecognised ticker names that import as the remedy. Exchange-less listings are unique by ticker. `PUT` returns `422` for an unrecognised token ticker, a Crypto listing *with* an exchange, a non-Crypto listing *without* one, or a duplicate exchange-less ticker; the two pairing refusals name which side is wrong ("a Crypto listing has no exchange …" / "this listing needs an exchange …") rather than quoting the constraint behind them. A Crypto listing also cannot be an `amit`: an AMIT is an attribution managed investment **trust**, and a crypto asset is not an interest in one (`amit` or `amit_from` on a Crypto listing is `422`) — left accepted it would make the [annual tax report](#annual-tax-report) permanently incomplete for want of an AMMA statement no coin issues, and refuse every income row on the listing that is not trust income. Trades and Sells on a Crypto listing auto-populate `settlement_date` as the trade date itself (same-day settlement — no T+n, no holiday calendar, no coverage warning), and crypto parcels flow through every report exactly like share parcels (AUD cost base/proceeds, the 12-month 50% discount, loss netting, holding-account transfers). Crypto closing prices are collected daily at the UTC-midnight cut-off into the [closing-price history](#closing-prices) (which feeds the [report snapshots](#report-snapshots)); ad-hoc report requests can still supply their own prices.

`DELETE` returns `422` while anything still references the listing — trades, income, AMMA statements, DRP enrolments, corporate actions, stored closing prices, renames — naming each blocking table and its row count (see [Deletes blocked by a dependant](#deletes-blocked-by-a-dependant)). **A listing that has ever had a closing price [entered by hand](#closing-prices) can no longer be deleted at all.** The two rules meet in a dead end: the listing's foreign key refuses while the price stands, and a manual price is stored `ok`, which `DELETE /closing_prices/:listing_id/:price_date` refuses in turn (only errored rows are deletable — a manual price is replaced by another manual `PUT`, never removed). This is deliberate on both sides and not a case the API works around: a hand-entered price is a deliberate, audited correction, and a listing anyone has priced by hand is one with real history. Rename it (`POST /listings/:id/rename`) or leave it in place rather than trying to delete it. (The one narrow exit: a manual price dated inside the listing's own [`unpriced_before`](#closing-prices) span *is* deletable, since nothing values it — but a listing carrying that marker is one with real history too.)

**Ticker or name changes:** a renamed security is the *same* security — its `id` is the identity everything references (trades, income, AMMA statements, DRP enrolments, corporate actions), and nothing is keyed by ticker, so the full history — parcels, cost bases, and acquisition dates (the 12-month discount clock) — stays attached across the rename regardless of how it's recorded. Don't create a new listing for a renamed security: that would start a second, unrelated history. (A relisting under a new entity via merger/takeover is a different event — a CGT parcel substitution, recorded as a [`ScripForScrip` corporate action](#corporate-actions) — not a rename.)

A listing with no recorded trades, income, or closing prices yet can still be edited freely with a bare `PUT` (e.g. fixing a typo'd ticker before anything is entered against it). Once it has any of those, a `ticker` or `exchange_mic` change must go through `POST /listings/:id/rename` instead — `PUT` returns `422` naming the endpoint. (`currency` is frozen by the same rule but has no rename path — see above.) This is a dated, audited event, not a bare field edit:

```
POST /listings/:id/rename
{ "effective_date": "2024-06-01", "ticker": "LAR", "exchange_mic": "XNYS", "name": "Lithium Americas Corp", "price_symbol": null, "note": "..." }
```

`effective_date` and `ticker` are required; `exchange_mic`, `name`, `price_symbol`, and `note` are optional. `exchange_mic` omitted keeps the listing's current exchange (a rename never needs to clear a non-Crypto listing's exchange — that would violate the exchange/security_type pairing, which a rename doesn't touch); `name` omitted keeps the current name; `price_symbol` omitted leaves it exactly as it was (it isn't part of the rename chain — an override that matched the old ticker rarely matches the new one, so it's carried over only when the request says so). The server derives `old_ticker`/`old_exchange_mic` — and `old_name`/`old_price_symbol`, the two fields a rename can change without them being part of the identity chain — from the listing's current row, never from the request, so the chain can't be falsified and the [undo](#ticker-or-name-changes) can put back everything the rename overwrote. Returns `201` with the created rename record, `404` for an unknown listing, or `422` if the request changes neither `ticker` nor `exchange_mic`, `effective_date` is not after the listing's most recent rename, `effective_date` is after **today**, the resulting ticker is already held by another listing (the refusal names that listing — its id and name — rather than quoting the `UNIQUE(exchange_mic, ticker)` constraint behind it; an exchange-less Crypto listing collides on the bare ticker alone, the basis of the `listings_crypto_ticker` partial index, since NULL exchanges compare distinct under the composite constraint), the rename would move the listing to an exchange **quoting a different currency** from the listing's own (see below), or — for a Crypto listing — the new ticker isn't a recognised digital-token code or the rename would give it an `exchange_mic` (a crypto asset trades on no MIC-coded venue).

`effective_date` is bounded at both ends, and the upper bound is today: a rename is applied to the listing **as soon as it is recorded** — there is no pending state, and no job that promotes a rename on its date — so a change entered ahead of its announced date would rename the security now while its own chain said the change had not happened yet. Every report would show the new ticker immediately, and the live quote (which resolves the provider symbol from the chain's *current* span) would fail until the effective date arrived, while daily collection — which resolves the symbol as at each date fetched — kept correctly using the old one. So a future `effective_date` is refused `422` naming the rule and today's date. **Record an announced change on the day it takes effect**; a rename dated today is accepted.

**A rename can move exchanges, but not across a currency boundary.** `exchanges.currency` is the money a market quotes in; a listing's own `currency` denominates every stored [closing price](#closing-prices) and is **frozen** once the listing has any recorded trades, income, or prices (above). A rename onto an exchange quoting other money would therefore leave a listing whose prices are collected from a market in one currency and stored as another — and, because the freeze then applies, one that can never be corrected in place: it becomes unpriceable (the provider either serves no such series, or serves one whose currency the [quote-currency cross-check](#closing-prices) refuses — an errored row either way). So it is refused `422`, naming both currencies and pointing at the same remedy the currency freeze does: **a new listing in the other currency plus a [transfer](#transfers) of the parcels to it** (a listing with nothing recorded against it yet can simply have its `currency` corrected with `PUT /listings/:id` first). Only a rename that **changes** the exchange is tested — one that leaves the listing where it is is never refused over a mismatch it did not introduce, and a listing already in that state (a plain `PUT` can create one: no write path compares the two columns) is reported by [Health](#health)'s `exchange_currency_mismatches` instead.

`GET /listings/:id/renames` returns the chain (`id`, `listing_id`, `effective_date`, `old_ticker`, `new_ticker`, `old_exchange_mic`, `new_exchange_mic`, `old_name`, `old_price_symbol`, `note`), newest first. `DELETE /listings/:id/renames/:rename_id` undoes a rename — restoring **all four** fields a rename can change, `ticker`/`exchange_mic`/`name`/`price_symbol`, from its `old_*` columns — but only the **newest** one for that listing (chain integrity: undo unwinds last-in-first-out). Returns `204`, `404` for an unknown rename, or `422` if it isn't the newest.

`price_symbol` is restored because it is not cosmetic: it drives [price collection](#closing-prices) verbatim for every date in the listing's *current* identity, which after an undo is the restored, older one — so an undo that left it behind went on fetching prices under a symbol that existed only because of the rename that was undone. A rename that *introduced* an override on a listing that had none is undone back to none: `old_price_symbol` is NULL there and that NULL is the real prior value, told apart from an unrecorded row by `old_name` (`name` is NOT NULL on a listing, so every rename recorded from migration 0040 on has one).

**A rename recorded before 0040 recorded neither**, and neither is recoverable from anything stored, so its undo behaves exactly as it did then — `ticker` and `exchange_mic` restored, `name` and `price_symbol` left standing. NULL in those columns means *unrecorded*, never "restore to null"; nothing is back-filled from the listing's current row, which would only make an undo claim to have reverted a listing to what it already is.

Every rename is audited via [row history](#row-history) (`listings` and `listing_renames` are both audited tables), so the full before/after is recoverable even without the chain. Reports show the **current** ticker throughout (a rename is the same security — the ATO-correct view), with two exceptions that resolve a ticker **as at** a date. The [Annual Tax Report](#annual-tax-report) prints every row's ticker as at that row's own date, so an archived document keeps reading the way the broker statement did at the time. The [listing activity ledger](#listing-activity) has no per-row ticker column — it is one listing's own history, and its own renames appear in it as dated `Ticker/exchange change` rows — but where a row names *another* listing (a scrip-for-scrip or demerger counterpart) it names it at the ticker that listing had on the action's own date, never one it was only renamed to later.

The recorded chain also drives **price collection**: fetching a date resolves the provider symbol — and the exchange calendar — from the identity that was in force *on that date*, so pre-rename history is requested under the pre-rename symbol automatically. See [Closing prices](#closing-prices).

## Holding accounts

Custody/location accounts within the one taxpayer — e.g. an employer share-plan account holding RSU-vested shares alongside a personal broker account — so the same listing can be held in several places at once with different treatment (notably [DRP enrolment](#drp-enrolments)). Account 1 (`Default`) is seeded by the migrations: every write that omits `holding_account_id` lands in it, so single-account users never see the dimension.

| Method | Path | Description |
|--------|------|-------------|
| `GET` | `/holding_accounts` | List all holding accounts |
| `GET` | `/holding_accounts/:id` | Get one holding account |
| `PUT` | `/holding_accounts/:id` | Create or rename a holding account |
| `DELETE` | `/holding_accounts/:id` | Delete a holding account |

`PUT` returns `422` for a duplicate `name` (UNIQUE). `DELETE` returns `422` if the account still holds data (trades, income, AMMA statements, DRP enrolment periods, or a transfer references it) or is the seeded default account — move or remove the data first.

## RBA FX rates

Monthly foreign exchange rates from the RBA's F11 table, stored as foreign-currency units per 1 AUD (so `AUD = foreign / rate`). Rows are created only by the import; the only other write is a correction to a stored rate.

| Method | Path | Description |
|--------|------|-------------|
| `GET` | `/rba_fx_rates` | List all FX rates (ordered by currency, then month) |
| `GET` | `/rba_fx_rates/:id` | Get one FX rate |
| `PUT` | `/rba_fx_rates/:id` | Correct one stored rate (see below) |
| `POST` | `/rba_fx_rates/import` | Trigger an import (see below) |

`POST /rba_fx_rates/import` is idempotent: it inserts new `(currency, month)` rows and leaves existing rows unchanged (re-running creates no duplicates). With an **empty body** it fetches the live RBA F11 CSV; with a **non-empty body** it imports that supplied CSV (useful for retries when the RBA endpoint is unreachable). Returns `200 OK` with `{ "inserted": N, "skipped": M }`, `422` if the feed can't be parsed, or `502 Bad Gateway` if the RBA fetch fails. Where the feed carries a **different** rate for a `(currency, month)` already stored, the stored row is still left alone — a scheduled import must never rewrite a figure a lodged return was computed at — but the disagreement is reported rather than being counted as a repeat: the response carries `"conflicted": [{ "id", "currency", "month", "stored", "feed" }, …]` (absent when empty) and each one is logged at warn level, since a scheduled run's response goes nowhere. Applying one is a deliberate act — `PUT /rba_fx_rates/:id` below. An import that inserted new rows also **trues up** the [provisional report snapshots](#report-snapshots) in the same run — regenerating each one, so a snapshot valued at a fallback-month rate is finalised the moment its real rate arrives — and the response then carries the regeneration summary as `"snapshot_true_up": { "regenerated": […], "blocked": […] }` (absent when nothing was inserted). The same import (and true-up) also runs on the cron schedule as the `rba-fx-import` job (see Jobs).

`PUT /rba_fx_rates/:id` **corrects one stored rate**: `{ "rate": "0.6512" }`, addressing a single row by id and carrying nothing else — a row's `(currency, month)` is its identity, and changing that would silently re-point every conversion that used it. Because the import never overwrites, this is the only way to fix a figure that landed wrong: a typo in a hand-pasted retry CSV, a truncated download, or an upstream revision the feed now disagrees with (the import's `conflicted` list names those rows and their ids). Returns `204 No Content`, `404` if no rate has that id, or `422` for a non-positive rate (every conversion divides by it). The write is **[audited](#row-history)** — `rba_fx_rates` is an audited table, so the superseded figure stays recoverable — and **stales every [report snapshot](#report-snapshots) dated on or after the first of that month**, since each was valued at the old rate. Correcting a rate changes tax figures for every record in that currency-month, so it is deliberately not something an import can do on its own.

## MIC registry

The ISO 10383 Market Identifier Code list, imported from the official ISO20022 `ISO10383_MIC.csv`. Reference data only — used to validate curated exchanges; rows are written only by the import, so the resource is read-only via `GET`.

| Method | Path | Description |
|--------|------|-------------|
| `GET` | `/mic_registry` | List all MIC entries (ordered by MIC) |
| `GET` | `/mic_registry/:mic` | Get one MIC entry |
| `POST` | `/mic_registry/import` | Trigger an import (see below) |

`POST /mic_registry/import` upserts every row in the feed in one transaction, tracking the latest ISO publication (a MIC's status/expiry can change), so re-running creates no duplicates and refreshes changed entries. With an **empty body** it fetches the live ISO CSV; with a **non-empty body** it imports that supplied CSV (useful for retries when ISO is unreachable). Returns `200 OK` with `{ "imported": N }`, `422` if the feed can't be parsed, or `502 Bad Gateway` if the ISO fetch fails. The same import also runs on the cron schedule as the `mic-import` job (see Jobs).

## Currencies

The recognised currencies list — fiat (ISO 4217) and digital tokens (ISO 24165) in one table. Rows are written only by the import, so the resource is read-only via `GET`.

| Method | Path | Description |
|--------|------|-------------|
| `GET` | `/currencies` | List all currencies (ordered by code) |
| `GET` | `/currencies/:code` | Get one currency |
| `POST` | `/currencies/import` | Trigger an import (see below) |

`POST /currencies/import` upserts every row from the feed in one transaction (idempotent — re-running creates no duplicates). The feed format is detected from its content: an **ISO 4217 XML** body (the SIX Group "List One") imports fiat currencies, an **ISO 24165 JSON** body (the DTIF registry snapshot) imports digital tokens. With an **empty body** it fetches the live sources: the ISO 4217 list (free), plus the ISO 24165 registry when the `DTI_REGISTRY_USER_ID` / `DTI_REGISTRY_PASSWORD` environment variables are set (the DTIF download requires Basic-auth credentials; the token fetch is skipped with a warning when they are absent, and fiat still imports). Returns `200 OK` with `{ "imported": N }`, `422` if the feed can't be parsed, or `502 Bad Gateway` if a fetch fails. The same import also runs on the cron schedule as the `currency-import` job (see Jobs).

## Closing prices

Daily closing-price history per listing, in the **listing's quote currency** (never AUD-converted — reports convert via the [FX rules](#fx-conversion) at read time). Rows are collected automatically by the scheduled `price-import` job (see Jobs): after each exchange's `close_time`, it stores the close of every trading day in the **last 14 calendar days** whose stored row is missing or errored — so a day lost to a host or provider outage is backfilled by the following runs instead of becoming a permanent hole. That window is deliberately the same length as the [report-snapshot](#report-snapshots) catch-up window: a date the snapshot job keeps retrying but collection no longer refills could never unblock itself. A listing marked [`unpriced_from`](#listings) is skipped from that date on — the provider serves nothing for it, so every call would only store another errored row. A listing counts as collectable if it was held on **any** day of the window, not only today — a holding sold part-way through it still needs prices for the days before the sale, which is what valuing a past snapshot date reads. Trading days only (weekends and the exchange's seeded [holidays](#exchange-holidays) store no row and are not an error), and days already stored ok are never re-fetched, so runs are idempotent. Exchange-less ([Crypto](#listings)) listings trade continuously, so their daily cut-off is **UTC midnight**: the stored price for date *D* is the daily candle completing at 00:00 UTC at the end of *D* (~10–11 am Sydney the next morning).

The provider behind the pluggable fetcher is **Yahoo Finance** (unofficial chart API, via the `yfinance-rs` crate): free and keyless, covering ASX (`.AX` suffix), NYSE/Nasdaq (plain ticker), and crypto (`<TICKER>-<currency>`) in one source. A **failed fetch is stored as an errored row** (`status: "error"`, `price: null`, `error` text) — never silently missing — and is replaced by a later successful re-fetch. Yahoo serves float32-precision values, so prices are rounded to 7 significant digits before storing. The symbol is resolved **as at the date being fetched**, from the listing's [rename chain](#listings): a range straddling a rename is split at the effective date and fetched with one provider call per identity, each under the ticker (and exchange suffix) in force over its own span. So pre-rename history is recovered under the pre-rename symbol without the caller naming it. Prices for other exchanges need a symbol mapping added to the fetcher first, or a **[`price_symbol` override](#listings)** set on the listing (used verbatim, ahead of the derived mapping) — until either is done their fetches store errored rows naming the exchange. `price_symbol` is the *current* provider spelling, so it applies only to dates in the listing's current identity; earlier spans use their own derived symbol. A **wrong, renamed or delisted symbol** is diagnosed rather than left to read like an outage, and the provider signals it two ways, both of which store a message naming the symbol and the remedy on every date of the window: a call that returns **zero candles across the whole requested window** (as opposed to a data gap on one date among others), and a call that **fails with the provider positively answering that it serves no such series** — Yahoo's `404 Not found`, or the `400` its chart API returns for a retired ticker, which is what the pre-rename half of a backfill straddling a rename actually meets. The verdict is read off the provider client's **typed** error (its HTTP status), never by matching words in a message. It is deliberately narrow: a `429`, a `5xx`, a timeout, a connection failure or any other `4xx` is **not** evidence about the symbol, so those dates store the provider's own error text unembellished — an outage misdiagnosed as a dead symbol would send you hunting for a rename that never happened. Both are judged per identity span, so the message names the symbol that actually failed, a span that did return candles still stores its prices, and the **advice matches the span**: for a date in the listing's current identity it names `price_symbol` (or an explicit `symbol` on backfill), and for an earlier span it names only the backfill `symbol` override — `price_symbol` is the current spelling and is never applied to a pre-rename date, so advising it there would be advice that cannot work. `GET /reports/health`'s `errored_prices` list (see [Health](#health)) surfaces every listing with any errored row, since a stuck symbol otherwise only shows up indirectly as a missing snapshot. A day the provider can **never** serve is priced by hand instead (`PUT /closing_prices/:listing_id/:price_date`), recorded with where the figure was sourced from and why it was needed. An **unbounded run** of such days — the provider serves nothing at all from a delisting or suspension onward — is not hand-priced day by day: set [`unpriced_from`](#listings) on the listing instead, which stops collection, quiets the health alarm, and has valuation carry the last stored close forward.

**A stored price is in its own trading day's unit basis** — the price the security actually traded at on `price_date`. The provider does not serve it that way: Yahoo restates a security's whole close series into the **current** basis the moment it splits (`auto_adjust(false)` turns off *dividend* adjustment only), so after a 10-for-1 it answers `120.888` for a day the security closed at `1208.88`, while the reports re-base parcel quantities into the snapshot date's *own* basis — multiplying the two used to put the valuation out by the split ratio. Which basis a figure arrived in is fixed by **when it was observed**, which `fetched_at` dates, so each row keeps the figure as observed (`price_as_observed`) and derives the stored one from it: `price = price_as_observed ×` the ratio of every [`ShareSplit`/`BonusIssue`](#corporate-actions) dated in `(price_date, fetched_at]`. That means a price fetched **after** a split is normalised on the way in, and recording, editing or deleting a split or bonus issue **re-derives** every affected stored price for that listing — in the action write's own transaction, so the two can never be committed out of step and the order they were entered in cannot matter. A series collected day by day *before* a split is left untouched by it (each fetch predates the event). Because the observation is kept, every re-derivation is a recompute from source rather than a delta applied to an already-adjusted number: it is idempotent and reversible. Two consequences worth stating: the restated figure carries only the provider's ~7 significant digits, so a ratio that does not divide out exactly recovers no more precision than that; and a re-fetch is no longer byte-identical to the provider's response. A re-base is an ordinary update of an [audited](#row-history) table, so the superseded figure stays recoverable and the snapshots valued at it are marked stale and regenerate. A database whose prices were stored before this rule existed is repaired once by the `price-rebase` job (see [Jobs](#jobs)).

**Which corporate actions restate the series.** The set is a strict *superset* of the actions that re-base quantities — the invariant above is not unconditional, and this is the whole of the exception list:

- [`ShareSplit`](#corporate-actions) and `BonusIssue` restate it, by the same ratio they multiply the unit count by. Each states its own ratio, so nothing more is needed.
- A [`Demerger`](#corporate-actions) restates it **too**, and this is the case the split rule does not cover: the provider applies a spin-off price-adjustment factor to the whole pre-demerger series exactly as it does for a split, while the demerger changes **no unit count** on the head listing (it issues units of a *different* one). There is therefore no ratio to read — `demerger_cost_base_pct` is an ATO cost-base apportionment, not a price ratio — so the factor is **derived** from a stated fact the action carries: `demerger_close_price`, what the security **actually closed at** on `demerger_close_date`, the last pre-demerger trading day, divided by the provider's own figure for that same day. Both sides are kept as facts and the quotient is computed at re-base time, so the close can be stated before any pre-demerger history is backfilled (and re-derives itself if that history is re-fetched); the two orders give the identical stored price. A demerger with **no** stated close restates nothing — its pre-demerger prices stay as the provider served them, silently at the current level, which is what [`GET /reports/health`](#health)'s `demergers_missing_close` names.
- `ScripForScrip` and `WorthlessShares` do **not**: both end the original ticker, so the provider stops serving a series rather than restating one — that is the [`unpriced_from`](#listings) case, not this one.
- `ReturnOfCapital`, `RightsIssue` and `BuyBack` do **not**: a distribution goes through the provider's dividend-adjustment channel, which `auto_adjust(false)` turns off, and neither of the other two is in the provider's adjustment set at all.

A derived demerger factor is an exact `Decimal` division done once per walk (numerators and denominators multiply first), but it is still a division: prices recovered through one carry no more than the provider's ~7 significant digits **and** whatever the division rounds off, so they are as accurate as the close you state, not exact.

**The one exception, and it is on the demerger's own date.** The re-base walk covers the rows dated **strictly before** the demerger: `price_basis_ratio` skips an event dated on or before the price date, so the demerger date's own row keeps the figure the provider served — which for a demerger is typically still spin-off-adjusted, the provider's adjustment ex-date being the day *after* the event. Leaving it is deliberate, not an off-by-one, because **on the demerger date the model already holds the demerged parcel** (the [demerge](#corporate-actions) writes the new listing's Buy dated that day). The head listing's price on that date must therefore stand for the *standalone stub*; un-adjusting the row would recover the **combined** entity and double-count the side already valued separately. The provider's adjusted figure is its own estimate of that standalone-equivalent, which is very nearly what is wanted. On Evan's Lithium Americas demerger (2023-10-03), the head listing's stored `6.517713` un-adjusts by the stated close's own factor, 16.85 ÷ 6.453301 = `2.611067`, to `17.01818` — the whole undivided company that day — while the demerged listing is already held at its when-issued `9.67`, so the pair would value the holding at **26.69** a unit against the previous day's 16.85, a 58% overnight jump; as stored the two sum to **16.19**, a 3.9% move. The market then priced the standalone entity at 6.01 and 6.09 on the next two trading days — much nearer the stored figure than the 7.348 a naive repair would have entered. A [`ShareSplit`](#corporate-actions) reaches the same boundary by the other route (on a split's effective date the price is *already* in the new basis), so the single rule is right for both kinds. [`GET /reports/health`](#health)'s `demergers_missing_close` counts `adjusted_days` by that same boundary, so the demerger-date row is not reported as needing re-basing — the check and the walk agree, and a row left in the derived basis is invisible to both by design.

A **hand-entered** price is contemporaneous **by declaration** — you are stating what the security traded at that day — so it is neither normalised on entry nor ever re-based; `price_as_observed` equals `price` for it. Enter the figure as it stood on the day, not a split-adjusted one from a current chart.

| Method | Path | Description |
|--------|------|-------------|
| `GET` | `/closing_prices` | List stored prices, newest first, **including errored rows**; filter with `?listing_id=`, `?from=`, `?to=` (dates inclusive) |
| `POST` | `/closing_prices/fetch` | Re-fetch one day for one listing (body: `{ "listing_id": 1, "price_date": "YYYY-MM-DD" }`) |
| `POST` | `/closing_prices/backfill` | Backfill a listing over a date range (body: `{ "listing_id": 1, "from": "...", "to": "...", "symbol": null }`) |
| `POST` | `/closing_prices/clear_unpriced_before` | Clear every stored row a listing's [`unpriced_before`](#listings) supersedes — the whole span before that date, in one transaction (body: `{ "listing_id": 1 }`) |
| `PUT` | `/closing_prices/:listing_id/:price_date` | Store a price **entered by hand** for a day the provider cannot serve (body: `{ "price": "62.48", "sourced_from": "...", "reason": "..." }`) |
| `DELETE` | `/closing_prices/:listing_id/:price_date` | Delete one **errored** row — the acknowledgement that no price will ever exist for that day — or any row inside the listing's [`unpriced_before`](#listings) span |

`POST /closing_prices/fetch` replaces whatever is stored for that (listing, day) — its purpose is re-running a failed fetch once the provider recovers. It returns `200` with the freshly stored row (which is itself errored if the provider failed again), `404` for an unknown listing, or `422` if the day's close is not final yet, the date is not a trading day, the date falls on or after the listing's [`unpriced_from`](#listings) or before its [`unpriced_before`](#listings) (the refusal names the marker and the two ways past it: enter the price by hand, or move/clear the marker), or the stored row was **entered manually** (see below — the refusal quotes the row's `reason`).

`PUT /closing_prices/:listing_id/:price_date` stores a closing price **entered by hand**, in the listing's quote currency (never AUD), with the provenance that makes it auditable: `sourced_from` (where the figure came from, e.g. `asx.com.au closing report`) and `reason` (why manual entry was needed, e.g. `provider serves no candle since the delisting`). Both are required and neither may be blank. This is the way out of a day the provider can never serve — a delisted or mis-served symbol, or a permanent hole in its series — which [valuation](#report-snapshots) otherwise blocks forever, taking that day's snapshots with it. The stored row is `status: "ok"` with `origin: "manual"` and `source: "manual"`, and every report reads it exactly like a fetched price. The day must be a trading day whose close is final, as for a fetch: a price on any other date is one nothing would ever read. A manual price may deliberately replace a stored provider price that is wrong; that is an ordinary update, so snapshots dated on or after the day are marked stale and regenerate at the corrected figure. Returns `204`, `404` for an unknown listing, or `422` for a non-positive price, a blank `sourced_from` or `reason`, a non-trading day, or a close that is not final yet.

Once stored, a manual price is **only ever replaced by another manual price**. The scheduled job and backfill skip it as they skip any day already stored ok; an explicit re-fetch is refused `422`; and it cannot be deleted (it is an ok row — see `DELETE` below). A hand-entered price is a deliberate correction for a day the provider got wrong or cannot serve, so the provider does not take the day back. Replacing one is recorded: `closing_prices` is an [audited table](#row-history), so the superseded price and the `sourced_from`/`reason` given for it stay recoverable — look the row up by the `id` each stored price carries.

`POST /closing_prices/backfill` fills a listing's history (e.g. after importing an old trade: trade date to today): trading days only, days already stored ok are skipped, and the missing days are fetched in one provider call — an expected trading day the provider returns no candle for (e.g. a historical holiday outside the seeded calendar) is stored as an errored row. The `to` date is clamped to the latest complete trading day. The optional `symbol` is a **one-off override for this fetch only** (not persisted to `listings.price_symbol`), for a provider spelling the rename chain doesn't record — a recorded rename needs no override, since the symbol is already resolved as at each date. Prices land under the listing's own `listing_id` either way, so history stays unified regardless of which symbol fetched it — but each stored row records the symbol it was fetched under (`fetched_symbol`, above), so an overridden run is not afterwards indistinguishable from an ordinary one. Nothing verifies that the symbol names the *same security*; the only automatic check is the quote currency (above). The `to` date is also clamped to the day before the listing's [`unpriced_from`](#listings), if one is set, so a range crossing it fills the priced part and stops, and the `from` date is clamped **up** to its [`unpriced_before`](#listings) for the same reason at the other end. Returns `200` with `{ "trading_days", "already_stored", "fetched_ok", "errored" }` (counted over the clamped range), `404` for an unknown listing, or `422` if `from` is after `to`, the range contains no complete trading day, `from` itself falls on or after `unpriced_from`, or `to` itself falls before `unpriced_before`.

**Every fetched row records the symbol it was fetched under** (`fetched_symbol`), ok rows and errored rows alike. It is recorded **always**, not only when it differs from the symbol the [rename chain](#listings) derives: recording it only on a difference would make `null` mean two different things — "fetched under the ordinary symbol" and "not recorded" — which is precisely the ambiguity the column exists to end. A backfill run with the one-off `symbol` override (below) therefore stays distinguishable from an ordinary fetch afterwards, and a re-fetch under a different symbol *replaces* the record along with the figure, so a row never claims a symbol it no longer came from. The value comes from the fetcher itself, so it is always in the namespace of the `source` beside it. It is `null` for a **hand-entered** price (nothing was fetched — the two are paired by a schema CHECK), for any row stored **before** the column existed (unrecorded, and not recoverable after the fact: nothing infers one from the ticker), and for the errored row a fetch stores when no symbol could be resolved at all — an exchange with no provider mapping, which the row's own `error` names. Nothing calculates from it: it is provenance, listed by `GET /closing_prices`, shown on the Closing Prices screen, and carried into the [audit trail](#row-history).

The symbol a fetch used is *not* a claim that it named the right security — the provider answers whatever series it has for that spelling, and two securities' series look alike. The one automatic cross-check is the **quote currency**: whatever symbol was asked for, a candle whose currency is not the listing's is refused and the day is stored as an **errored row** naming both currencies, exactly as any other provider failure for that date is — so a foreign-currency figure is never stored as if it were the listing's own, and the reason is on the record. That catches a symbol that reached another market; it does not catch one that reached another security quoted in the same currency, which is why the symbol is recorded rather than merely checked.

`POST /closing_prices/clear_unpriced_before` clears every stored row a listing's [`unpriced_before`](#listings) marker supersedes — every date before it, `ok` rows included — in **one transaction**, answering `{ "listing_id", "unpriced_before", "deleted" }`. It takes **no date range**: the span is read from the listing's own declaration, so this can never become a general bulk-delete of price history. It exists because a span of hundreds of borrowed days is not a runbook one `DELETE` at a time (the case it was written for is 635 rows). Safe to re-run — a second call reports `deleted: 0` — and nothing is destroyed: `closing_prices` is [audited](#row-history), the `AFTER DELETE` trigger fires once per row, so every cleared figure and its `sourced_from`/`reason` stay recoverable. Prices of *other* listings on the same dates are untouched, as are the listing's own rows on and after the marker (including the close an [`unpriced_from`](#listings) run carries forward, which is by definition inside the served span). Returns `200`, `404` for an unknown listing, or `422` for a listing that declares no `unpriced_before` — without one there is no superseded span, and an ordinary price is replaced, never deleted.

Each stored row also carries `price_as_observed`: the figure as the provider served it (or as it was entered), which `price` is derived from per the basis rule above. A fetched row also carries **`fetched_symbol`**: the provider symbol it was actually fetched under, in `source`'s namespace — see the paragraph below. Each stored row carries an `id`: a server-assigned surrogate key that exists so the row can be looked up in the [audit trail](#row-history) (`{"table": "closing_prices", "row_id": <id>}`). Every write addresses a row by its `(listing_id, price_date)` natural key — the `id` is never used to address one, and is preserved when a price is replaced, so one row's whole revision history sits under one id.

`DELETE /closing_prices/:listing_id/:price_date` removes a single **errored** row: the acknowledgement that no price will ever exist for that (listing, day), so it stops being reported by `GET /reports/health`'s `errored_prices` (see [Health](#health)) — which otherwise nags forever about a row no re-fetch can fix. The two cases that need it are a date **before the security's first trading day** (a backfill reaching back past the listing date) and a **permanent hole in the provider's series** (a real trading day the provider simply has no candle for). An **ok** row is rejected `422`: real price data is replaced by a re-fetch (`/fetch`, `/backfill`) — or, for a manual row, by another manual `PUT` — never deleted, so this endpoint can never punch a hole in a valued series — which is also why deleting needs no snapshot-staleness handling (an errored date was never valued). For a listing actually held on that date, deleting the row does *not* unblock its snapshot — [valuation](#report-snapshots) still refuses the date, now for want of any row at all — it only clears the standing alarm.

**The one relaxation: a date inside the listing's [`unpriced_before`](#listings) span**, where an `ok` row is deletable whatever its origin — hand-entered or fetched. The refusal exists to stop a hole being punched in a **valued** series, and inside that span there is no valued series to hole: the marker declares that no price is obtainable for the security before that date, so it **supersedes** every stored row for those days — [valuation](#report-snapshots) leaves the holding out of the totals instead of pricing it, and even the `unpriced_from` carry-forward is floored at the marker. Deleting there is the acknowledgement that the stored figure never was a valuation, which is what lets a span priced from another security's series be retired (see `POST /closing_prices/clear_unpriced_before` above for the bulk form). Deleting still needs no staleness handling: setting or moving the marker is itself what stales the affected snapshots, so by the time the rows go, the dates have already been regenerated without them — and clearing the marker later stales them again, after which regeneration reports each date blocked for want of a price, which is the truth once the rows are gone. The mirror marker [`unpriced_from`](#listings) gets **no** such relaxation and this asymmetry is deliberate: a date on or after *that* one **is** valued — at the last stored `ok` close, carried forward — so deleting a row there could change a valuation or remove the very figure being carried.

Returns `204`, `404` if no row is stored for that listing and date, or `422` for an ok row outside any `unpriced_before` span.

## Report snapshots

Stored daily results of the three price-dependent reports — [portfolio overview](#overview), [unrealised gains](#unrealised-gains), and [performance](#performance) — one stored row per (report, date). The scheduled `report-snapshot` job (see Jobs) runs daily after the last relevant close and **catches up over a bounded window**: it computes the latest calendar date every held listing can be valued at with final prices (typically yesterday, once the prior NYSE close and the crypto UTC-midnight cut-off are in), then generates every missing snapshot date — including a hole an earlier blocked date left behind — from the series' first stored snapshot, capped at 14 calendar days back, up to that latest date, and regenerates any stale or provisional snapshot in the window. A date still blocked (a missing or errored price) is skipped with its blocker in the job's failure detail and retried on later runs, so a late price *delays* that date's snapshot instead of losing it. Each listing is valued at its nearest trading day on or before the snapshot date — a weekend or holiday date uses the prior close — with the stored quote-currency price converted to AUD per the [FX rules](#fx-conversion).

A snapshot records the report *as at its date*: facts dated after the snapshot date (trades, sales, income, corporate actions, AMIT adjustments by their statement's year end) are excluded, and recording a **back-dated fact** marks every snapshot dated on or after it **stale** — atomically with the fact write, via database triggers no write path can bypass. A stale snapshot keeps returning its stored rows, flagged, until regenerated (the job regenerates stale window dates itself); regeneration re-runs the report with the stored prices and the new facts. A day on which any held listing's price fetch failed (or was never fetched) has **no snapshot at all** — missing, distinguishable from stale — until the price re-run or backfill succeeds and the day is generated on demand or by the job's window.

**Carried-forward prices:** a held listing marked [`unpriced_from`](#listings) — the provider has stopped quoting it — is valued at its **last stored ok close** from that date on, rather than blocking the whole portfolio's snapshot for that date forever. The snapshot is stored flagged **`price_carried_forward`**, with the affected report rows carrying `price_carried_forward: true`, so the substitution is never silent. This is deliberately **not** the `provisional` flag: that one marks an interim FX rate a later import trues up, and both true-up runs (`POST /report_snapshots/regenerate_provisional` and the post-FX-import regeneration) target provisional dates — a carried-forward price never clears, so folding the two together would turn a bounded true-up into one that regenerates the same dates forever. Nothing retries a carried-forward snapshot: the daily job leaves it alone, and *clearing* `unpriced_from` marks every snapshot from that date on stale, which is what puts the real prices back.

**Excluded holdings:** a held listing dated **before** its [`unpriced_before`](#listings) — the day the price provider's series begins — has no obtainable price at all, so it is **left out** of that date's totals rather than blocking them. The snapshot is stored flagged **`holding_excluded`** with **`excluded_holdings`**, a list of `{ "listing_id", "ticker", "reason" }` naming each absent holding, and the affected report rows are left unvalued carrying the same reason as `price_unavailable`. Nothing is substituted — that is the difference from a carried-forward close, which is a real price once observed — so the stored total is **smaller than the portfolio by exactly those holdings**, and the [series](#report-snapshots) graph **steps** where the excluded listing's own series begins. A third flag rather than a reuse of either other one: `provisional` is what the true-up runs select on and an excluded holding never clears (folding it in would make that pass unbounded), while `price_carried_forward` says the figure rests on an interim input where this one says the figure is missing a holding. Nothing retries it; *clearing* `unpriced_before` marks every snapshot dated before it stale, which is what puts the holding back. A date on which **every** held listing is excluded is **blocked**, not stored empty: a total missing every holding is zero of zero, and storing it would draw a false floor through the series — the 422 names each excluded holding.

**Provisional snapshots:** the RBA publishes a month's FX rate only after the month ends, so a current-month snapshot of a non-AUD holding cannot use its own month's rate. Instead of failing, generation values it at the most recent earlier month's rate (at most 2 months back — beyond that it fails loudly) and stores the snapshot flagged **`provisional`**, with the affected report rows carrying `fx_provisional: true`. Provisional is distinct from stale: the facts are right, the rate is an interim one. When an [FX import](#rba-fx-rates) lands new rates it regenerates every provisional snapshot in the same run (the true-up), and the daily job regenerates provisional window dates too — a regeneration whose conversions all used real-month rates clears the flag. Only these valuation paths ever use a fallback-month rate; tax calculations and FY reports keep the strict [FX rules](#fx-conversion).

| Method | Path | Description |
|--------|------|-------------|
| `GET` | `/report_snapshots` | List stored snapshots (metadata only: `report`, `snapshot_date`, `generated_at`, `stale`, `provisional`, `price_carried_forward`, `holding_excluded`, `excluded_holdings`), oldest first; filter with `?report=`, `?from=`, `?to=` |
| `GET` | `/report_snapshots/series` | The graphable time series: per snapshot date, the portfolio's AUD totals (`market_value`, `total_cost_base`, `unrealised_gain`) plus the `stale`, `provisional`, `price_carried_forward` and `holding_excluded` flags and the `excluded_holdings` naming what a stepped total omits, oldest first |
| `GET` | `/report_snapshots/:report/:date` | One snapshot's metadata plus its stored report rows (exactly what the live report returned at generation) |
| `POST` | `/report_snapshots/generate` | Generate — or regenerate a stale/provisional — day's snapshots (body: `{ "date": "YYYY-MM-DD" }`; omit the date for the latest fully-valuable day) |
| `GET` | `/report_snapshots/regenerate_range` | The default `regenerate_all` bounds — `{ "from", "to" }`, each `YYYY-MM-DD` or `null` if nothing has ever been held — for the UI to prefill the range boxes |
| `POST` | `/report_snapshots/regenerate_all` | Regenerate every date in `[from, to]` that anything was held on (bulk repair after back-dated edits, and a backfill for dates that never had a snapshot); body `{ "from", "to" }` optional, either or both `null`/omitted defaulting per `regenerate_range` above (`from` is then clamped up to the first-ever-held date); per-date blockers are reported, unblocked dates still regenerate |
| `POST` | `/report_snapshots/regenerate_provisional` | Regenerate only the provisional snapshot dates — the manual counterpart of the post-import true-up. A `price_carried_forward` date is **not** included: nothing trues one up |
