- **`missing_rate`** — the record's conversion month has no imported [RBA rate](#rba-fx-rates). `resting_on` says what the amount converts at meanwhile: `spot_override` (a deliberate transaction-date rate, so nothing is missing — importing the month will not change it), `record_fx_rate` (the record's own manual fallback, applied *silently* — a trade left at a placeholder `1` costs a US$15,000 parcel at A$15,000), or `nothing` (income, an AMMA statement, a return of capital — every report that has to convert it answers `422` until the month is imported). This is the gap [health](#health) cannot see: it reports only the newest imported month across all currencies, so a hole in the middle of a series is invisible there. Records whose conversion is already covered by a row that *is* listed are not repeated — an [inheritance](#inheritances) and an [ESS vest](#vesting-an-ess-statement) resolve their rate once and carry it onto the parcel Buy they create.
- **`settlement_crosses_rate_month`** — a non-AUD trade contracted in one rate month and settled in another, where the **CGT event K10/K11** omission becomes visible (see [Known limitations](#known-limitations)). Both legs convert at the contract month, so the currency movement over the settlement window is not computed; inside one month it is nil by construction and nothing is reported.
- **`reduction_converted_at_acquisition_month`** — a non-AUD parcel taking an AMIT (CGT event E10) or [return-of-capital](#corporate-actions) (G1) reduction from a different month, the case the **cost-base FX timing** limitation describes. Where both months' rates are imported the `detail` names them both, so the row says what the difference actually costs rather than only that one exists. An AUD parcel is never listed — with one currency there is no conversion for the month to matter to.

The third member of that family — LPR expenditure on a foreign inherited parcel — is *refused* at write time instead, because there the amount has nowhere correct to go at all; these two produce a defensible figure, so they are reported rather than blocked.

### Settlement holiday coverage

```
GET /reports/settlement_holiday_coverage
```

Flags every trade whose stored settlement date cannot be trusted, on two independent questions — **non-blocking** on both: trade writes are never rejected, and an explicit `settlement_date` is a deliberate override the user is asserting, so a flagged row stays editable and is left exactly as entered.

- **Was the window computed against a complete calendar?** The `[date, settlement_date]` window is checked against the seeded holiday coverage for the trade's exchange (see [Exchange holidays](#exchange-holidays)); outside it, settlement-date calculation silently degrades to weekend-only skipping, so the date may be wrong if the exchange observes a holiday in the window.
- **Is the settlement date itself a day the exchange traded?** The stored `settlement_date` is put to the listing's own trading calendar as it stood **on that date** (the same `is_trading_day` test `PUT /closing_prices/:listing/:date` and the [trade](#trades) date rule use). A settlement on a Saturday or a public holiday is wrong by construction whoever wrote it — the auto path cannot produce one on a complete calendar, so in practice it is a hand-entered override with a typo in it.

Returns one record per affected trade (sorted by ticker, then date, then trade id) with fields: `trade_id`, `listing_id`, `ticker`, `mic`, `trade_type`, `date`, `settlement_date`, `coverage_status` (`outside_holiday_coverage` = the window extends beyond the seeded years, `no_holiday_coverage` = the exchange has no seeded holidays at all, `inside_holiday_coverage` = the window was computed against a complete calendar, so the row is listed for the second question alone), the exchange's coverage span `coverage_start`/`coverage_end` (1 Jan of the earliest seeded holiday's year to 31 Dec of the latest's; null when there is no coverage), and `settlement_non_trading_reason` (`weekend` or `holiday` when the settlement date is not a trading day, else null — a row can carry both this *and* an out-of-coverage `coverage_status`, which is why they are two fields rather than two values of one). After an exchange change the calendar the settlement date is judged on is the one in force at settlement, which need not be the `mic` the listing carries today.

Trades that are inside coverage **and** settle on a trading day are omitted — an empty report means every settlement window sits inside a seeded calendar and every stored settlement date falls on a day its exchange traded. It does **not** mean each stored date is what today's calendar would compute: seeding a missing holiday year clears the coverage alerts for that year without recomputing the settlement dates that were computed while it was missing, and one of those stays wrong unless it now lands on a closed day, where the second question catches it. **Run the [`settlement-recompute` job](#jobs) after seeding a calendar** — it re-derives the settlement dates the server computed itself, which is what makes the seeding actually repair them (a `settlement_date` you supplied is never rewritten, so a deliberate override survives the run). Trades on exchange-less ([Crypto](#listings)) listings are skipped entirely — they settle same-day with no holiday calendar, so there is no coverage to be outside of and no closed day to land on (the [exchange MIC validation report](#exchange-mic-validation) likewise has nothing to validate for them: it checks curated exchanges, and a Crypto listing has none).

### Tax-deferred E4 cross-check

```
GET /reports/e4_cross_check
```

Flags every trust [income](#income) row carrying a non-zero `tax_deferred_amount` whose listing has **no** `ReturnOfCapital` [corporate action](#corporate-actions) dated in the row's financial year — for a non-AMIT unit trust the statement's tax-deferred amount is a CGT event E4 cost-base reduction (`docs/ato/cgt-non-assessable-payments.md`), modelled as the `ReturnOfCapital` action, so a recorded amount with no matching action means the cost base is silently overstated. **Non-blocking**: income writes are never rejected — this only surfaces reductions still to be entered. The row's financial year is its assessment year (the governing `entitlement_date` when set, else `date_paid` — the [tax summary](#tax-summary)'s attribution rule). Returns one record per affected row (sorted by ticker, then date paid, then income id) with fields: `income_id`, `listing_id`, `ticker`, `date_paid`, `tax_deferred_amount` (verbatim, in the record's currency), and `tax_year` (the FY the action is expected in, identified by the calendar year of its 30 June end). Rows whose listing has a same-FY action, and rows with no or a zero amount, are omitted — an empty report means every recorded tax-deferred amount has its E4 reduction entered; entering the matching action clears a row. (AMIT funds are unaffected: their cost-base movement is driven solely by the AMMA `cost_base_adjustment` — see [AMMA statements](#amma-statements) — and any tax-deferred figure on an AMMA statement stays informational.)

### AMIT cash cross-check

```
GET /reports/amit_cash_cross_check
```

Flags every financial year in which an AMIT listing received cash distributions ([cash-only income rows](#income)) into a holding account that has **no [AMMA statement](#amma-statements)** of its own whose `tax_year_end_date` falls in that year. AMIT cash rows are excluded from the [tax summary](#tax-summary) — the AMMA attribution is the assessable record — so a cash year with no AMMA entered would silently drop that income from the return. **Non-blocking**: income writes are never rejected — this only surfaces statements still to be entered; entering the fund's AMMA clears the row. The converse is not flagged: an AMMA year with no cash rows is fine (the fund can be held without receiving or recording cash that year), and non-AMIT listings are never this report's business. Each row's financial year is its assessment year (the governing `entitlement_date` when set, else `date_paid` — the tax summary's attribution rule), and only the years the listing was an AMIT are asked about: a fund that converted has no AMMA statement for its pre-[`amit_from`](#listings) years and is not flagged for them.

Coverage is asked **per holding account**, because the statement is issued per holder account: a fund held in two accounts is issued two AMMA statements, each attributing only its own account's units (which is also why an [AMIT adjustment](#amit-adjustments) may only touch its statement's account, and why [generation](#generating-amit-adjustments) narrows to it). One account's statement therefore clears only that account's row; the other account's income stays flagged until its own statement is entered. Returns one record per affected (listing, year, account) triple (sorted by ticker, then year, then account) with fields: `listing_id`, `ticker`, `tax_year` (the FY the AMMA is expected for, identified by the calendar year of its 30 June end), `holding_account_id` (the account the cash was paid into, and so the account whose statement is missing), `cash_rows` (how many cash income rows that account has in the year), and `cash_total_aud` (the year's gross cash components in AUD — the income that would go unreported). An empty report means every AMIT cash year has its attribution entered, in the account the cash was paid into.

### AMIT adjustment cross-check

```
GET /reports/amit_adjustment_cross_check
```

Flags every [AMMA statement](#amma-statements) whose per-parcel [AMIT adjustment](#amit-adjustments) **set** does not reconcile to the statement. Each row is validated in isolation at write time (Buy/DRP, matching listing and holding account, quantity within the parcel, no repeated parcel), but nothing at write time can see the set: a missed parcel silently overstates the cost base of the units it covers, and an unnecessary one over-reduces it — and because CGT event E10 floors the reduced cost base at nil, an over-reduction can manufacture a capital gain that was never made. **Non-blocking**: nothing is rejected; [generating the set](#generating-amit-adjustments) from the statement clears a row.

Four checks, all reported on one row per flagged statement:

- **no adjustments at all** on a statement whose `cost_base_adjustment` is non-zero — the whole statement's cost-base effect is missing. A statement with a nil per-unit figure adjusts nothing and is not flagged;
- **coverage mismatch** — Σ of the adjusted quantities outside the band `units_held` … `units_held + the units disposed of during the statement's year`, reported with the signed shortfall or the excess above the band. The upper allowance is there because a row may legitimately cover units **sold during the year**: s 104-107B makes the adjustment just before the end of the income year *or just before a relevant CGT event* (LCR 2015/11 para 13). For a holding sold out or transferred away mid-year that is the only kind of unit there is — the statement then states nil units held, and the correct hand-entered rows would otherwise read as excess coverage forever. Below the band a parcel is missing; above it one is duplicated or covered for more units than it held. The comparison is **split-aware** in both terms: adjustment quantities are in each parcel's as-acquired units and `units_held` is in the statement year's basis, so each quantity — and each disposal counted into the allowance — is re-based to the year end before summing (a naive comparison would false-positive on any split);
- **duplicate parcel** — the same parcel adjusted more than once on one statement (rejected at write time now, so this only fires on rows entered before that rule);
- **parcel outside the statement's year** — the two unambiguous cases only: the parcel was acquired *after* `tax_year_end_date`, or it was fully sold before 1 July of that financial year. A parcel disposed of *during* the year was genuinely held for part of it and is never flagged.

Returns one record per flagged statement (sorted by ticker, then year) with fields: `amma_statement_id`, `listing_id`, `ticker`, `tax_year` (the FY the statement attributes, identified by the calendar year of its 30 June end), `holding_account_id`, `units_held` (the statement's own figure, verbatim), `units_adjusted` (Σ re-based into the statement year's unit basis), `parcel_count`, and `problems` — the list of problems found, each a self-contained sentence. Statements whose set reconciles are omitted, so an empty report means every statement's adjustments reconcile.

### Rollover consistency

```
GET /reports/rollover_consistency
```

Flags every [transfer](#transfers), [scrip-for-scrip exchange](#exchanging-a-scrip-for-scrip-takeover), and [demerger](#demerging) whose **stored** figures no longer match what today's facts say they should be. Those three operations write the cost base (and units) their replacement parcels carry as a *stored* value, computed from the source parcels, splits, [AMIT adjustments](#amit-adjustments) and [return-of-capital](#corporate-actions) payments as they stood when the operation ran; nothing re-derives them afterwards. So a fact behind the operation that changes later moves the parcels the reports still walk while the frozen replacement figures stay put — and the same holding reports a different cost base depending only on the order things were entered. The two loudest routes into that state are refused at write time (a split/bonus/return of capital dated on or before an operation that has already run, and an AMIT adjustment covering units one carried away); this report covers the rest — a **source parcel's own price, brokerage, or quantity edited after the move**, an AMMA statement's per-unit figure corrected, or any state that predates those guards. **Non-blocking and advisory**: nothing is rejected, and the fix is to delete the operation and run it again so the replacement parcels carry the current figures.

What is compared: Σ of what the consumed units' cost base is **now** — through the same `domain::rollover` call the operations themselves make, so this report cannot disagree with them about the pipeline — against Σ of the replacement parcels' stored cost base, **per currency** (a replacement parcel keeps its source parcel's currency, and amounts are never netted across currencies). That is exact for a transfer, for a demerger (whose `demerger_cost_base_pct` only *splits* the carried total across the head and demerged parcels, so the sum still has to match), and for a cash-free scrip exchange. Units are compared for a **transfer** only, where they move one for one; the other two apply their own ratios. A **partial-rollover scrip exchange** (one carrying a cash component) is listed as *not checked*, saying so in its own `problems` entry: how much of each parcel's cost base went to the cash side is the exchange's own apportionment, and re-deriving it here would be a second copy of the operation — a false mismatch on every such exchange would be worse, and so would silence.

Returns one record per flagged operation (oldest first) with fields: `kind` (`Transfer` | `ScripForScrip` | `Demerger`), `group_id` (the `transfers.id` or `corporate_actions.id` the group hangs off), `date` (the operation date), `listing_id`, `ticker` (the listing whose parcels were consumed), `sell_trade_id` (the closing Sell, so the group opens without a search), `replacement_trade_ids`, and `problems` — each a self-contained sentence naming what the operation carried, what those units are worth now, the difference, and the recovery. Operations that reconcile are omitted, so an empty report means every rollover's stored figures still hold. The [annual tax report](#annual-tax-report)'s completeness section carries these rows too, **unfiltered by year**: a rollover from an earlier year is exactly the one whose stale figure this year's disposals are costed on.

### Wash sales

```
POST /reports/wash_sales
```

Flags the wash-sale fact pattern the ATO warns may have the capital loss cancelled under Part IVA (TR 2008/1, `docs/ato/wash-sales.md`): every **loss-realising Sell** — any of its allocations realised a capital loss per the [realised gains](#realised-gains) report, so the rollover/transfer exclusions and the full adjusted-cost-base pipeline apply unchanged — with a **Buy or DRP of the same listing dated within the window either side**, across **all holding accounts** (a repurchase in another account of the same beneficial owner changes nothing economically). **Non-blocking and advisory**: writes are never rejected, the flagged loss still counts in every CGT report, and the pattern is not unlawful per se — TR 2008/1's own Example 6 is a repurchase 3 days later that survives Part IVA because it was market-driven, while Example 2's planned 24-hour round trip fails. Whether Part IVA could apply is a facts-and-circumstances judgment the report leaves to the taxpayer; it only makes the pattern visible.

The optional JSON body `{"window_days": n}` sets the scan window (`n ≥ 1`; `422` otherwise); absent or an empty body defaults to **30 days** — a review convention, not an ATO bright line (the ruling has no statutory window). Returns one record per loss-Sell × nearby-Buy pair (a Sell with several nearby Buys yields several rows; no pairs ⇒ an empty report) with fields: `sale_trade_id`, `listing_id`, `ticker`, `sale_holding_account_id`, `sale_date`, `capital_loss` (the sale's realised loss across its loss-making allocations, AUD, positive), `buy_trade_id`, `buy_holding_account_id`, `buy_date`, `buy_quantity`, and `days_apart` (`buy_date − sale_date`; negative = acquired before the sale). Provenance Buys that merely continue or relocate an existing holding never match (holding-account transfer-ins, scrip-for-scrip replacements, demerger Buys, inheritance Buys); rights-exercise and ESS-vesting Buys do — they are genuine new acquisitions. Symmetrically, a [transfer](#transfers)'s network-fee disposal Sell is never a candidate — the disposal is compelled by the transfer (a dominant non-tax purpose, timed by the transfer, with the fee units never re-acquired), so it is not the TR 2008/1 fact pattern; its capital loss still counts in every CGT report. Matching is otherwise by date pattern only: the Buy a Sell drew its own allocations from can match, and the report does not judge purpose.

### Franking at-risk

```
GET /reports/franking_at_risk
```

Explains the [tax summary](#tax-summary)'s otherwise-silent franking-credit denials: one record per dividend whose entitled shares fail the at-risk holding-period walk (45 days, 90 for a `preference` listing; LIFO identification — the same `docs/ato/you-and-your-shares-dividends.md` rule the tax summary applies, computed by the same shared code so the two cannot disagree), plus one per dividend the walk could not be anchored to at all. **Non-blocking**: nothing is rejected; an empty report means every attached credit is claimable **on the tests this report models** — the 45/90-day at-risk count and the small-shareholder exemption. It is an all-clear for those: a dividend the walk cannot anchor is listed as `untested_no_ex_date` rather than passing silently, so no dividend leaves the report untested. An AMIT's cash rows are not candidates (they carry no attributable credits — the fund's credits arrive on its [AMMA statement](#amma-statements), which has no per-distribution ex-date to walk), but that too follows the [listing](#listings)'s status **in the dividend's own financial year**: a converted fund's pre-conversion distributions are ordinary trust income, so they are tested here and their credits count toward the year's A$5,000 total — which can be what tips the year's *other* dividends out of the small-shareholder exemption. The other two conditions of being a **qualified person** — the **30%-at-risk test** (hedges, options and futures) and the **related payments rule**, which the small-shareholder exemption does not excuse — are not modelled and no recorded fact could test them, so an empty report assumes the holdings are unhedged and under no related-payment obligation (see [Known limitations](#known-limitations)). Fields: `income_id`, `listing_id`, `ticker`, `tax_year`, `date_paid`, `ex_date` (the date the walk is anchored on — see [Income](#income): the recorded ex-date, else a trust row's entitlement date, else the payment date), `ex_date_recorded` (false when that is the payment-date fallback), `required_days` (45/90), `window_end` (the qualification window runs from `ex_date` to here — a disposal inside it with fewer than `required_days` at-risk days disqualifies; one after it cannot), `entitled_units`, `disqualified_units`, `credits_attached` (AUD), `credits_at_risk` (the disqualified share of the credits per the walk), `credits_denied` (what the tax summary actually excludes — equal to `credits_at_risk`, or zero under the exemption), and `status`:

- `denied` — the walk disqualified units and the tax summary excludes `credits_denied`. The `denied` rows sum, per financial year, to the tax summary's `franking_credits_denied`.
- `exempt_small_shareholder` — the year's attached credits are under A$5,000, so the rule doesn't apply. The row still explains the failing walk, since the exemption is year-wide and lapses as more credits arrive.
- `untested_no_ex_date` — credits are attached but no entitlement date was recorded (no `ex_date`, and no `entitlement_date` on a trust row), so the walk fell back to the payment date and found nothing: a disposal between the real ex-date and the payment is invisible to it. Nothing is denied on such a walk, and the row denies nothing itself — it is here so that an *empty* report can be read as an all-clear. Record the ex date (or the trust distribution's entitlement date) and the row resolves into a real answer. A buy-back's dividend component is never untested: its `date_paid` is the tender date, which is when the entitlement was fixed.

### Health

```
GET /reports/health
```

Operational health / data-freshness in one read (a single read transaction), driving the web UI's cross-view banner: a failing import or silently aging price/FX data is surfaced on every screen rather than only when the Jobs page is opened. Returns `{ "latest_price_date", "prices_stale", "latest_fx_month", "fx_stale", "failed_jobs", "errored_prices", "unpriced_days", "demergers_missing_close", "demergers_head_not_continuing", "duplicate_price_series", "duplicate_actions", "duplicate_amma_statements", "duplicate_income", "duplicate_interest", "duplicate_expenses", "duplicate_ess_statements", "duplicate_inheritances", "exchange_currency_mismatches", "ess_30_day_rule", "non_trading_day_trades" }`:

- `latest_price_date` — the newest [closing price](#closing-prices) date stored with status `ok` across every listing (`null` when none). `prices_stale` is true when it is more than **3 business days** (a coarse Mon–Fri count — deliberately not the per-exchange holiday calendar; this is a freshness alarm, not a settlement calculation) before the server's current date.
- `latest_fx_month` — the newest imported [RBA FX rate](#rba-fx-rates) month, `YYYY-MM` (`null` when none). `fx_stale` is true when it is older than the previous calendar month (the RBA publishes month M's F11 rates shortly after M ends, so an older latest month means the weekly import has stopped landing new data).
- `failed_jobs` — every job whose **most recent** recorded run failed, as `{ "name", "finished_at", "error" }` (sorted by name); a job that failed and then succeeded is recovered and not listed.
- `errored_prices` — every listing with at least one errored [closing price](#closing-prices) row **inside the span its provider serves it** — dated before its [`unpriced_from`](#listings) and on or after its [`unpriced_before`](#listings) — newest error first: `{ "listing_id", "ticker", "errored_days", "latest_errored_date", "latest_error" }`. A wrong, renamed, or delisted provider symbol otherwise only shows up indirectly, as a missing snapshot from the errored date onward (`stored_valuations` refuses to value a date with an errored price) — this is the surface that names it directly. Fix the symbol (`price_symbol` on the listing, or record the rename via [`POST /listings/:id/rename`](#listings)) then re-fetch via `/closing_prices/backfill` or `/fetch`. When no re-fetch can ever succeed — a date before the security's first trading day, or a permanent hole in the provider's series — clear the row with [`DELETE /closing_prices/:listing_id/:price_date`](#closing-prices) instead, so the list stays a to-do rather than a permanent nag. When **no** re-fetch will ever succeed *again* — the provider has stopped quoting the security altogether — set [`unpriced_from`](#listings) on the listing: rows from that date drop off this list (they are expected, not a to-do) and valuation carries the last close forward instead of blocking. When the provider has never quoted the security that far *back* — its series begins later — set [`unpriced_before`](#listings) instead: rows before that date drop off for the same reason, and valuation leaves the holding out of those dates' totals. Rows inside the served span are real holes and stay reported.
- `unpriced_days` — every listing with a **held day that has no stored [closing price](#closing-prices) row at all**, oldest hole first: `{ "listing_id", "ticker", "unpriced_days", "earliest_date", "latest_date" }`. The missing-row counterpart of `errored_prices`, and the two lists **partition** the problem: a day whose stored row is errored is reported by `errored_prices` only, a day with no row by `unpriced_days` only. An errored fetch at least leaves a row to find; a day nobody ever fetched is silent and permanent — it surfaces only as a snapshot stuck stale, and by the time it is noticed the provider may no longer serve that far back. It happens when a trade is entered later than the `price-import` job's 14-day lookback on a listing not otherwise held (a batch of statements entered years after the fact), so nothing ever attempted those days. A day counts as unpriced when it is exactly what [valuation](#report-snapshots) would ask for and not find: for a calendar date the listing was held (the same as-at-that-date holding rule the valuation path uses, so a fully-sold listing stops being reported after its sale and a sold-then-rebought one is covered for both spans), that date's **valuation day** — its nearest trading day at or before, on the calendar in force *then*, so a rename's earlier exchange calendar applies to the dates before it — has no row. Days whose close is not final yet (today's, an unsettled crypto candle) are never reported, and a non-trading day is not a hole (it values at the previous trading day). `unpriced_days` counts **distinct valuation days**, so a weekend and the Friday it values at are one hole. Days from the listing's [`unpriced_from`](#listings), and days before its [`unpriced_before`](#listings), are excluded for the same reason `errored_prices` excludes them — the first unconditionally, since setting that date requires a stored ok price before it, so a marked listing always has a close to carry forward, and the second because nothing can ever be fetched there at all. Ordered oldest hole first because the oldest is the least recoverable. Close it with `/closing_prices/backfill`, or a manual `PUT` for a day the provider can never serve — deliberately **not** auto-filled: closing a hole stays a deliberate act, since a silently auto-filled hole is how a wrong series gets in.
- `demergers_missing_close` — every recorded [`Demerger`](#corporate-actions) with **no stated pre-demerger close** whose head listing holds pre-demerger [closing prices](#closing-prices) the provider served **after** the demerger, newest demerger first: `{ "action_id", "listing_id", "ticker", "demerger_date", "adjusted_days", "earliest_date", "latest_date", "manual_days", "manual_earliest_date", "manual_latest_date" }`. Those rows carry the provider's spin-off price adjustment and nothing can undo it until the action states what the security actually closed at on the last pre-demerger trading day — so every valuation of those dates is silently at the *current* level. This is the one price problem no other list here sees: the rows are `ok`, not errored, so `errored_prices` and `unpriced_days` both pass over them, and the only symptom is a valuation that looks plausible and is wrong. A day observed *before* the demerger is not counted (it arrived contemporaneous), nor is a day after it (the provider never restated those). **`adjusted_days` is what a stated close repairs, not the size of the suspect span**, and `manual_days` (with `manual_earliest_date`/`manual_latest_date`, `null` when it is 0) is the rest of it: the `ok`, **hand-entered** prices dated before the demerger, whenever they were entered, which the re-base walk skips by design — a manual price is contemporaneous by declaration, so a stated close leaves those exactly as typed. The span as a whole therefore starts at the earlier of `earliest_date` and `manual_earliest_date`; on Evan's LAC the fetched half is 260 rows from 2022-09-20 while the span is 635 from 2021-03-25. The manual figure is **context on this warning, not a warning of its own**: a demerger with only hand-entered pre-demerger prices is not listed (nothing needs re-basing), and stating the close clears the row while those prices stay as they are — judging a hand-entered price wrong is a different question from this one. Clear it by adding `demerger_close_date`/`demerger_close_price` (and their provenance) to the action — the write re-bases the listing's prices in its own transaction, and the entry is auditable because the arithmetic is derived from the stated figure rather than typed in as a ratio. **Non-blocking, and deliberately not a constraint**: a provider that did not adjust for a particular spin-off, or a series fetched entirely before it, needs no statement at all.
- `demergers_head_not_continuing` — every recorded [`Demerger`](#corporate-actions) whose **head listing looks like the entity the demerger created** rather than the one that continued, newest demerger first: `{ "action_id", "listing_id", "ticker", "demerger_date", "head_unpriced_before", "head_first_price_date", "head_held_from", "demerger_listing_id", "demerger_ticker", "demerged_priced_days", "demerged_earliest_date", "demerged_latest_date" }`. A demerger has a continuing entity (the head, `listing_id`) and one it *creates* (`demerger_listing_id`), and nothing in the model records which is which beyond those two columns — so entering them **swapped** is accepted in full. No tax figure moves (the apportionment percentages attach to the tickers, not to the roles) and no write-time check has anything to test; what breaks is the price side, because the head parcel then sits on a listing that did not exist when it was bought, so the provider serves no history for it and every valuation before the demerger is unvaluable. Evan's Lithium Americas demerger was recorded this way for three years: 921 snapshot dates could not value the holding, and 635 prices were borrowed from the other listing to paper over the gap. **The predicate is an asymmetry, not an absence** — a head with no earlier prices proves nothing on its own, since a series nobody ever collected looks identical. All three of these must hold: the head listing holds **no `ok` closing price of any origin** dated before the demerger (`head_first_price_date`, its earliest stored `ok` price, is therefore on or after it, or `null`; `head_unpriced_before` is the [`unpriced_before`](#listings) marker when it carries one — the explicit record of the same thing, corroborating the finding rather than required for it); the **demerged** listing holds `demerged_priced_days` ≥ 1 `ok`, provider-**fetched** prices dated *before* it (`demerged_earliest_date`/`demerged_latest_date`), which only the continuing entity can have; and the head listing was **acquired before** the demerger (`head_held_from`, its earliest Buy/DRP), so the model really does claim a security on days its own provider says it did not exist. Each clause kills a class of false positive: without the second, a database where nothing has been collected lights up on every demerger it holds (the evidence has to come from the provider, on the *other* side, and fetched rather than hand-entered — a hand-entered row is the operator's own assertion, and this check exists to question the operator's model); without the third, a demerger whose head history was simply never backfilled reads as a defect. **Is there a legitimate demerger of this shape?** The continuing entity traded before the event by definition, and the demerged one is created by it, so both halves of the inversion are already odd; the one case where a demerged listing genuinely predates the event — an in-specie distribution of an already-listed holding — has the parent trading throughout, so the first clause fails and nothing is reported. **What it does not catch**: it names the condition only once the borrowed rows are gone, or were never added. On the live data as it stood before the 2026-08-21 cleanup this check would have stayed **silent**, because the head listing did have pre-demerger prices — 635 of them, copied from the other listing. `duplicate_price_series` is what names those while they are there and this is what names the modelling error underneath once they are cleared: the two are **complements**, and between them the incident is visible at both stages. **Non-blocking, and deliberately not a constraint**: the remedy is to re-record the action with the sides swapped (and move the parcel's Buy to the continuing listing), a correction only the operator can judge against the company's own demerger notice. It clears as soon as the head listing is the one with the earlier series.
- `duplicate_price_series` — every pair of listings whose stored [closing prices](#closing-prices) are **identical across a long run of consecutive shared trading days**, newest run first: `{ "listing_id", "ticker", "other_listing_id", "other_ticker", "identical_days", "earliest_date", "latest_date", "fetched_days", "manual_days", "other_fetched_days", "other_manual_days" }` (the pair ordered by id, so it is reported once). One security's price series stored under **both** listings — what a series fetched or copied under the wrong symbol looks like once the fetch is over. Like `demergers_missing_close` this is a price problem nothing else here can see (every row is `ok`, so `errored_prices` and `unpriced_days` pass over them and the figures look plausible), and it is the **only** signal left by a row fetched under `/closing_prices/backfill`'s one-off `symbol` override before [`fetched_symbol`](#closing-prices) existed, which records nothing about where it came from. **The predicate is a run, not a total**: two distinct securities do close at the same figure by chance now and then, so `identical_days` counts the longest unbroken sequence of *comparisons* — days on which both listings hold an `ok` price — and a match elsewhere in history is not part of it. Thirty such days (about six weeks of trading) is the threshold; a day only one listing has a price for is not a comparison, so it neither breaks the run nor counts towards it (the run's span can therefore be longer than `identical_days`), while a day they hold and **disagree** on ends it. Prices are compared as decimals, not as stored text, so `10.5` and `10.50` match. A run whose closes **never move** is not reported however long it is — two instruments pinned to one figure (a pair of stablecoins at 1.00, two funds at a constant unit price) match forever without either series being a copy, and an alarm nobody can clear is worse than none. `fetched_days` / `manual_days` split each side's rows in the run the way `demergers_missing_close` splits its span, and for the same reason — the two halves need different remedies: a hand-entered row states where it came from and why (`sourced_from` / `reason`), a fetched one may state nothing at all. On Evan's data the check answers one row — listings 7 (`LAC`) and 8 (`LAR`), **634** consecutive identical days from 2021-03-25 to 2023-09-29, of which 375 of LAC's are hand-entered and 259 fetched — and nothing else across every other pair, including the one day (2024-02-08) the two really did both close at 4.12. **Non-blocking, and there is no way to silence it**, the same call as the rest of this list: it clears when the borrowed rows go — [`DELETE /closing_prices/:listing_id/:price_date`](#closing-prices), or the bulk [`POST /closing_prices/clear_unpriced_before`](#closing-prices) — or when they are replaced by the listing's own series. Setting [`unpriced_before`](#listings) deliberately does **not** quiet it: that marker says the provider serves nothing before the date, which is a reason to delete what is stored there, not to accept it — unlike `errored_prices`, where the marker explains an *absence*.
- `duplicate_actions` — every (listing, action type, date) carrying **more than one** [corporate action](#corporate-actions), newest first: `{ "listing_id", "ticker", "action_type", "date", "action_count", "action_ids" }` (`action_ids` ascending, so the surplus row can be opened and deleted without a search). Two actions of the same type, listing and date are two independent events to every reader, and the effect **compounds silently**: two identical `ReturnOfCapital` rows reduce each parcel's cost base twice, two identical 2-for-1 `ShareSplit` rows turn 100 units into 400. Both are plausible double entries — a re-submitted form, a re-imported statement — and both restate every cost base and quantity of the listing. **Non-blocking, and deliberately not a uniqueness constraint**: a genuine same-day pair exists in principle (two tranches of one capital return), so the pair stays enterable and this names it for you to judge. Clear it by deleting the surplus action (`DELETE /corporate_actions/:id`, rejected `422` while a trade still references it — undo the operation's trades first).
- `duplicate_amma_statements` — every (listing, financial year, holding account) carrying **more than one** [AMMA statement](#amma-statements), newest year first: `{ "listing_id", "ticker", "tax_year", "holding_account_id", "statement_count", "statement_ids" }` (`statement_ids` ascending, so the superseded row can be opened and deleted without a search). The attribution-side counterpart of `duplicate_actions`, and it compounds just as silently: the [tax summary](#tax-summary)'s `amma_*` lines and the [net capital gain](#net-capital-gain) report's gain buckets each sum **every** statement of the year, and because the one-adjustment-per-parcel UNIQUE index is per *statement*, each statement can also generate its own full set of [AMIT adjustments](#amit-adjustments) — reducing every parcel a second time. The usual cause is an **amended statement entered as a new row** instead of over the original. **Non-blocking, and deliberately not a uniqueness constraint** (the same call as `duplicate_actions`): a genuine pair exists in principle — a registry change mid-year, or a fund merger, leaves two part-year statements for one account — and an amended statement is easier to check against the original while both are enterable. The holding account is part of the key because the statement is issued per holder account: a fund held in two accounts legitimately has two statements for one year. Clear it by deleting the superseded statement (`DELETE /amma_statements/:id`, rejected `422` while its AMIT adjustments are still stored — delete those first, or regenerate the surviving statement's set with `"replace": true`).
- `duplicate_income` — every (listing, holding account, `date_paid`) carrying **more than one** [income](#income) row of **identical amounts**, newest first: `{ "listing_id", "ticker", "holding_account_id", "date_paid", "currency", "gross_amount", "income_count", "income_ids" }` (`income_ids` ascending, so the surplus row can be opened and deleted without a search; `gross_amount` is the franked + unfranked + foreign-source gross each row declares, in `currency`, naming the distribution rather than only its date). The distribution-side counterpart of the two above, and it double-counts just as silently: the [tax summary](#tax-summary)'s dividend lines, the franking credits behind them, the foreign income and the FITO limit are each summed row by row, so a re-submitted form or a re-imported statement declares the payment twice. **Non-blocking, and deliberately not a uniqueness constraint** (the same call as `duplicate_actions`): two dividends from one company on one day are legitimate in principle — an ordinary and a special dividend — so the pair stays enterable. That legitimate pair is exactly why **the amounts are part of the key**: (listing, account, date) alone would flag it, while requiring every money figure and the currency to match as well leaves only what is almost certainly one payment entered twice (an ordinary + special pair on one day is reported only if the two also pay identical amounts). Non-money differences — an `ex_date` filled in on one row only — are ignored, since that is how a re-entry usually differs from the original. Amounts are compared as decimals, not as stored text, so `70.0` and `70.00` match. Clear it by deleting the surplus row (`DELETE /income/:id`, rejected `422` while a DRP reinvestment is still linked — undo the reinvestment first).

- `duplicate_interest` — every group of **identical** [interest income](#interest-income) rows, newest first: `{ "date_paid", "currency", "amount", "source", "holding_account_id", "interest_count", "interest_ids" }` (`interest_ids` ascending). Interest has no listing to key on, so the free-text `source` ("ANZ savings account") and the optional holding account carry the payer identity that `listing_id` carries above — two $250 credits on one day from two different banks are legitimate and stay unflagged, while the same credit keyed twice doubles the [tax summary](#tax-summary)'s `interest_income` (or `foreign_interest_income`) line and any withholding beside it. Every stored field but the id is part of the key, including `foreign_source` and both withholding figures. **Non-blocking, and deliberately not a uniqueness constraint** (the same call as `duplicate_income`): a payer really can credit the same amount twice in one day — two equal term deposits maturing together — so the pair stays enterable. Amounts are compared as decimals, not as stored text, so `250.0` and `250.00` match. Clear it by deleting the surplus row (`DELETE /interest_income/:id`).
- `duplicate_expenses` — every group of **identical** [investment expense](#investment-expenses) rows, newest first: `{ "date_incurred", "expense_type", "currency", "amount", "description", "listing_id", "ticker", "holding_account_id", "expense_count", "expense_ids" }` (`expense_ids` ascending; `ticker` is `null` for a portfolio-wide expense). The deduction is claimed once per row, so a re-submitted form lifts the year's `deductions_*` line and lowers `net_assessable_investment_income` by the same amount again. The key is everything that identifies the expense — the date, the type, the money figures (the `gross_amount` / `deductible_percentage` provenance pair included: two rows agreeing on what is claimed but not on the gross it was apportioned from came off different invoices), the currency, the description, and both optional attributions — so two advice fees of $200 on one day against different listings, or with different descriptions, are ordinary entry. **Non-blocking, and deliberately not a uniqueness constraint**, the same call as the three above. Clear it by deleting the surplus row (`DELETE /investment_expenses/:id`).
- `duplicate_ess_statements` — every (listing, holding account, `taxing_point_date`) carrying **more than one** [ESS statement](#ess-statements) of **identical figures**, newest first: `{ "listing_id", "ticker", "holding_account_id", "taxing_point_date", "currency", "quantity", "discount_total", "statement_count", "statement_ids" }` (`statement_ids` ascending, so the superseded row can be opened and deleted without a search; `discount_total` is the D + E + F + pre-2009 discount each statement declares, in `currency`, naming the grant rather than only its date). The employee-share-scheme side of the same double entry, and it compounds on **both** sides at once: the [tax summary](#tax-summary)'s Item 12 labels (and the $1,000 taxed-upfront reduction computed over them) sum statement by statement, and each statement [vests](#vesting-an-ess-statement) its **own** cost-base-reset parcel — so a $1,000 grant of 100 shares entered twice reports $2,000 of discount and 200 shares held. The [30-day rule](ato/ess-30-day-rule.md) makes this the expected accident rather than a hypothetical: the employer issues an **amended** statement for one vest, and a user who enters it as a new row instead of over the original has exactly this shape. **Non-blocking, and deliberately not a uniqueness constraint** (the same call as `duplicate_income`): two vests on one date from different grants are ordinary, so the pair stays enterable — which is why **the figures are part of the key**, down to the `fx_rate` and the statement-AUD overrides: two same-date statements differing in quantity, market value or any discount label are two grants, not one entered twice. The derived `vest_trade_id` is **not** part of the key — whether the surplus statement has been vested yet says nothing about whether it is a duplicate. Amounts are compared as decimals, not as stored text, so `1000.0` and `1000.00` match. Clear it by deleting the superseded statement (`DELETE /ess_statements/:id`, which removes its vest Buy in the same transaction — rejected `422` while that Buy is drawn on by a Sell allocation or AMIT adjustment).
- `duplicate_inheritances` — every (listing, holding account, `date_of_death`) carrying **more than one** [inheritance](#inheritances) of **identical figures**, newest death first: `{ "listing_id", "ticker", "holding_account_id", "date_of_death", "currency", "quantity", "cost_base_total", "inheritance_count", "inheritance_ids" }` (`inheritance_ids` ascending; `cost_base_total` is the whole figure each row carries onto its parcel — first element + LPR expenditure — in `currency`). The deceased-estate side of the same double entry, and the one that doubles a **holding** rather than a year's income: each inheritance creates its own parcel Buy, so 100 units entered twice are 200 units held at twice the cost base in every open-parcels, valuation and realised-gains figure, and unlike the income-side duplicates no financial year bounds the error — it persists until the parcel is sold. **Non-blocking, and deliberately not a uniqueness constraint**: two inheritances of one listing from one death are ordinary (two holding accounts, two estates, or a part interest recorded in stages), so **the figures are part of the key** — two rows differing in quantity, cost base, LPR expenditure, rule or rate are two inheritances, not one entered twice, and the same units and figure recorded under *different* cost-base rules are shown, since that is a contradiction rather than a duplicate. Amounts are compared as decimals, not as stored text. Clear it by deleting the surplus inheritance (`DELETE /inheritances/:id`, which removes its parcel Buy in the same transaction — rejected `422` while that Buy is drawn on).
- `exchange_currency_mismatches` — every listing whose own `currency` is **not** the currency its exchange quotes in (`exchanges.currency`), by ticker: `{ "listing_id", "ticker", "currency", "exchange_mic", "exchange_currency" }`. Such a listing can no longer be priced — every fetch resolves the exchange's symbol, and the provider either serves nothing for it or serves a series whose currency the [quote-currency cross-check](#closing-prices) refuses, an errored row either way — and once it has any history its `currency` is frozen, so it cannot be corrected in place: the remedy is a new listing in the right currency plus a [transfer](#transfers) of the parcels to it. [`POST /listings/:id/rename`](#ticker-or-name-changes) refuses to *create* the state, but a plain `PUT /listings/:id` can still enter a listing in it — no write path compares the two columns — so it is reported here wherever it came from. Deliberately a warning rather than a `PUT`-time constraint: a real exchange can quote more than one currency (the LSE quotes GBp, USD and EUR) while `exchanges.currency` is a single column. Exchange-less ([Crypto](#listings)) listings have no exchange to disagree with and never appear.
- `ess_30_day_rule` — every disposal of ESS-vested shares **within 30 days after** the statement's taxing point, newest sale first: `{ "sale_trade_id", "listing_id", "ticker", "sale_date", "units_sold", "ess_statement_id", "vest_trade_id", "taxing_point_date", "days_after", "currency", "statement_discount", "statement_tax_year", "disposal_tax_year", "disposal_kind" }`. The one entry on this list that is a **date pattern** rather than a double entry, advisory in the way [wash sales](#wash-sales) are. Under the **30-day rule** (`docs/ato/ess-30-day-rule.md`, QC 23058 Example 11; ITAA 1997 s 83A-115(3)) a disposal inside that window **moves the deferred taxing point to the disposal date**: the discount is re-measured at what the sale realised, the CGT cost base resets to that same figure on that same date — so **there is no separate capital gain** — and the discount can land in the **next financial year** (`disposal_tax_year` ≠ `statement_tax_year`, which is exactly Example 11). Entering the employer's *original* statement and then the sale therefore gets two figures wrong in two different years at once: the original discount assessed in the original year, plus a capital gain that does not exist. The pairing is per allocation, so a Sell drawing on two vest parcels inside their windows is two rows — each statement is amended separately — and `units_sold` is the units that allocation consumed, not the whole vest. `days_after` is always 1..=30: a sale **on** the taxing point is never listed, since the rule is a no-op there (the taxing point already *is* the disposal date) and that is what the corrected entry looks like. **Non-blocking, and nothing is rewritten**: the correction is an amended employer statement — the employer must issue one within 30 days of becoming aware of the disposal — and this tool can neither know whether one was issued nor perform a re-measurement the ATO puts on the employer. Clear it by entering the amended statement **over** the original (`PUT /ess_statements/:id` with taxing point = the disposal date and market value = what the disposal realised; delete the vest Buy first, since a vested statement is frozen), then re-vesting. **What counts as a disposal** (`docs/ato/ess-takeovers-and-restructures.md`, ITAA 1997 s 83A-130): a [holding-account transfer](#transfers)'s closing Sell is **not listed** — the same beneficial owner holds the same interests before and after, so nothing is disposed of (which is why no CGT event arises either) and s 83A-130 does not reach it, and that move — vested shares out of an employer plan account into a personal broker account — is the ordinary use of the feature. The alert **follows the rollover chain**, so a real sale of those units after the move *is* listed against the statement they came from, even though the parcel sold carries no `ess_statement_id` of its own. A scrip-for-scrip exchange or demerger closing Sell **is** listed, with `disposal_kind: "TakeoverOrRestructure"` (an ordinary sale is `"Sale"`): s 83A-130(2) normally treats matching replacement interests as a **continuation**, so the taxing point does not move — but that rests on facts this tool does not record ((4)'s ordinary-shares test, (9)'s continuing-employment and two 10% tests), and (5) does treat a partial-rollover's **cash** component as a disposal to that extent. Where one rollover moved several parcels at once, its replacements descend from all of that operation's sources — the data records no finer link — so such a sale can be listed against more than one statement.
- `non_trading_day_trades` — every trade dated on a day its own exchange did not trade, newest first: `{ "trade_id", "trade_type", "listing_id", "ticker", "date", "exchange_mic", "reason", "source" }`, where `reason` is `"weekend"` or `"holiday"` and `source` says which write path created the row (`an ESS vest`, `an inherited parcel`, `a DRP reinvestment`, `a rights exercise`, `a buy-back participation`, `a scrip-for-scrip exchange`, `a demerger`, `a holding-account transfer`, `a worthless-shares recognise`, or `entered directly`). The calendar is resolved **as at the trade date**, so a listing that has since [changed exchange](#listings) is judged on the market it really traded on; exchange-less ([Crypto](#listings)) listings trade every day and never appear. The trade date is the CGT event date — it sets the 12-month discount clock, the financial year the gain falls in, and the day the T+n count starts from — which is why [`PUT /trades/:id` and `PUT /sells/:id` refuse one outright](#trades). This list is the safety net under that rule: the derived paths above write their trade rows directly and are exempt on purpose (a taxing point, a date of death and a corporate action's effective date need not be market days), and rows entered before the rule existed surface here too. **Non-blocking, and nothing is rewritten** — correct the trade, or the fact behind it (re-vest the [ESS statement](#ess-statements), re-date the [inheritance](#inheritances), re-perform the operation), whichever is wrong. This entry is about the **trade** date only; the same calendar question about a trade's stored `settlement_date` is answered by the [Settlement holiday coverage](#settlement-holiday-coverage) report, where a supplied override is flagged rather than refused.

A database with no prices or FX rates at all reports `stale = false` for that series — nothing has decayed, so a fresh install shows no banner; an import that breaks before ever succeeding surfaces through `failed_jobs` instead.

```
POST /reports/franking_at_risk/what-if
```

The contemplated-sale mode, surfaced next to the Sell flow in the web UI: body `{"listing_id": n, "sale_date": "YYYY-MM-DD", "units": "n"}` (all required; non-positive units ⇒ `422`) re-runs the holding-period walk for every franked dividend of the listing with that hypothetical Sell injected (in date order, after any recorded same-day trades), and returns the dividends whose denial would **grow** — nothing is written. Fields: `income_id`, `listing_id`, `ticker`, `tax_year`, `ex_date`, `required_days`, `window_end` (selling after this date can no longer disqualify the dividend — the lever for timing the disposal), `credits_attached`, `disqualified_units_now`, `disqualified_units_after_sale`, `additional_credits_at_risk` (the walk's denial with the sale minus without it, AUD), and the same `status` as above. A sale dated after every window end, or one that disqualifies nothing, returns an empty report.

### Row history

```
POST /reports/row_history
```

The read side of the **append-only audit trail** (`row_history` in [SCHEMA.md](SCHEMA.md); aligns with the ATO record-keeping guidance in `docs/ato/cgt-keeping-records-shares.md`): database triggers record the prior row whenever an audited table's row is updated or deleted — in the writing transaction itself, so no write path can bypass it, a rejected write leaves no phantom entry, and cascade deletes are recorded too. Entries are kept forever and the trail itself cannot be rewritten (append-only, database-enforced), so an accidental edit to a historical fact — which would silently change prior-year cost bases and tax figures — can be noticed and reconstructed.

Body `{"table": "trades", "row_id": 1}` (both required): `table` must be one of the audited tables (the `row_history.table_name` enum in SCHEMA.md; anything else ⇒ `422` naming the valid list). Returns the row's recorded history **newest first**, one JSON object per prior version: `history_id` (the trail entry's own id), `operation` (`UPDATE` | `DELETE`), `changed_at` (RFC 3339 UTC, millisecond precision), followed by every column of the audited table with its pre-write value (for `attachments` the `content` BLOB is excluded — `filename`/`byte_size`/`checksum` still identify the file). A row never updated or deleted returns `[]` — INSERTs are not recorded, so an empty trail means the row still reads exactly as first entered.

Audited tables are the user-entered ones whose values feed a calculation. [`closing_prices`](#closing-prices) is among them: a price can be **entered by hand**, and replacing one manual price with another would otherwise discard the superseded figure along with the sourcing and reason recorded for it. Its `id` is the `row_id` to ask for. So is [`rba_fx_rates`](#rba-fx-rates), for the same reason and since the same moment — a stored rate became correctable, and correcting one changes every tax figure in that currency-month, so the superseded rate stays recoverable.

# Known limitations

Deliberate scope decisions (2026-06-07), documented rather than modelled:

- **Taxpayer entity type** — all tax figures assume an **Australian-resident individual**: the 50% CGT discount and the 50% LIC capital gain deduction. The rates for other entity types (SMSF/complying super 33⅓%, company 0%, trust/partnership flow-through taxation) are not modelled. Every tax-summary, net-capital-gain and realised-gains row carries the assumption in its `taxpayer_basis` field, and the [parcel optimiser](#parcel-selection-optimiser) — which *ranks* its candidate strategies on the 50% discount — states it once on its response.
- **Cost base elements** — only cost-base elements 1 (acquisition) and 2 (incidental costs: brokerage + GST) are captured. Element 3 (ownership/holding costs), element 4 (capital improvements), and element 5 (title/defence costs) are not recordable — for listed shares they rarely apply, and element-3 borrowing costs are typically claimed as deductions instead (which excludes them from the cost base anyway). Consequently the ATO **reduced cost base** (used for capital losses; excludes element 3) is identical to the cost base by construction and is not modelled separately. See `docs/ato/cgt-cost-base.md`.
- **One taxpayer** — all holdings belong to a single taxpayer. [Holding accounts](#holding-accounts) partition custody/location (e.g. employer share plan vs personal broker) within that one taxpayer; a taxpayer-level ownership dimension (Individual / Joint / SMSF / Family Trust, each a separate CGT taxpayer) is not modelled.
- **Rights issues** — the modelled case is rights **issued free to the holder over post-CGT original shares**, exercised ([exercise](#exercising-a-rights-issue)) or disposed of ([sell rights](#selling-or-lapsing-rights), which also covers a renounceable-offer retail premium per TR 2017/4). **Pre-CGT original shares** (the market-value uplift on exercise; the disregarded gain on sale), **non-renounceable-offer retail premiums** (an unfranked dividend per TR 2012/1 — enter as [income](#income)), and **rights acquired beyond the holding's own entitlement** are not modelled. On the last: `rights_cost` records what was paid for rights that were exercised or sold, so rights *bought on-market* are supported up to the entitlement the holding earned at the record date — but not past it. Both the [exercise](#exercising-a-rights-issue) and [sell rights](#selling-or-lapsing-rights) endpoints cap their cumulative units at that shared entitlement and answer `422` beyond it, so a purchase of extra rights has nowhere to be recorded; enter the shares it produced as an ordinary [Buy](#trades) at their full acquisition cost instead. See `docs/ato/rights-issues.md`, `docs/ato/retail-premiums.md`.
- **Rollovers assume the rollover was chosen** (2026-08-15) — the two parcel-substituting corporate actions model **only** the rollover case: [`ScripForScrip`](#corporate-actions) is Subdiv 124-M with scrip-for-scrip rollover chosen, and [`Demerger`](#corporate-actions) is Div 125 with demerger rollover chosen. Recording either *is* the taxpayer's assertion that the rollover applies — nothing checks eligibility, and there is no no-rollover variant of the operation. That matters most for the **discount clock**: with rollover, the replacement/new interests carry the consumed parcel's acquisition date as their `deemed_acquisition_date`, so a parcel sold months after the event still discounts off the *original* acquisition (the ATO's demerger Example 32, `docs/ato/demergers.md`). **Without** rollover the answer is the opposite — the new interests are acquired at the exchange/demerger date under the ordinary cost-base rules and run their own 12-month clock from it (Example 33), and a takeover without rollover is simply an ordinary market-value disposal. Neither is modelled: enter a no-rollover exchange as a manual Sell plus Buy, and a no-rollover demerger's new interests as a manual [Buy](#trades) dated the demerger date (its capital-return component is a `ReturnOfCapital` action; an assessable demerger dividend is [income](#income)). **Pre-CGT original interests** are not modelled anywhere (see *Pre-CGT holdings* below), so the mixed pre/post-CGT apportionment those examples turn on cannot arise here. See `docs/ato/demergers.md`, `docs/ato/takeovers-and-scrip-for-scrip.md`.
- **DRP partial participation** — [enrolment](#drp-enrolments) is all-or-nothing per (listing, holding account): a registry plan that reinvests only a portion of a holding's units is not modelled. Stating the partial allotment as the reinvest `units` is refused (`422`), since it does not spend the distribution's cash. **Workaround:** split the distribution into two [income](#income) rows — one for the cash that was reinvested and one for the cash that was paid out — and reinvest the first. The parcel is then costed at the dividends actually applied to it, which is the ATO's rule (`docs/ato/cgt-dividend-reinvestment-plans.md`), and the year still declares the whole distribution, since the two rows sum to it. Two caveats: leave the `amount_per_security` / `securities_held` cross-check off both rows (neither half reconciles against the whole holding), and an exactly half-and-half split trips the [health report](#health)'s duplicate-income warning — same listing, account, `date_paid` and identical amounts is what that check looks for — so the deliberate pair reports as a suspected duplicate.
- **Employee share schemes** — both sides of an ESS interest are modelled: the assessable discount via [ESS statements](#ess-statements) (the Item 12 labels, reaching the [tax summary](#tax-summary)) and the cost-base-reset Buy via the [Vest](#vesting-an-ess-statement) operation (or entered manually as a Buy at the vest-date market value). The residual limits are: **unvested grants are not tracked** (they are not shares), and the $1,000 taxed-upfront reduction's **≤A$180,000 income test is answered by you, not computed** — the tool applies the de-minimis but can't see the taxpayer's whole income position. It is recordable rather than merely documented: set `ess_taxed_upfront_reduction_eligible: false` for the year in [tax year settings](#tax-year-settings) and the [tax summary](#tax-summary) reports that year's discount unreduced (see the [tax summary](#tax-summary)).
- **The ESS 30-day rule is flagged, never applied** (2026-08-18) — selling ESS interests **within 30 days after** the deferred taxing point moves the taxing point to the disposal date: the discount is re-measured at what the sale realised, the cost base resets to the same figure, there is **no separate capital gain**, and the discount can move into the next financial year (`docs/ato/ess-30-day-rule.md`, QC 23058 Example 11; ITAA 1997 s 83A-115(3)). The tool **detects the pattern and says so** — `ess_30_day_rule` on the [health report](#health), and a banner on every screen — but it does **not** re-measure anything: the discount is what your [ESS statement](#ess-statements) says it is. That is deliberate, not a shortcut. The re-measurement is a calculation the ATO puts on the **employer**, who must issue an **amended ESS statement** within 30 days of becoming aware of the disposal, and no stored fact here says whether one was issued — rewriting your stated discount would be inventing the employer's answer. **What to do:** enter the amended statement **over** the original (taxing point = the disposal date, market value = what the disposal realised), not as a second row — a second row is a [duplicate statement](#health) and reports as one. The corrected entry then produces the ATO's figures with no further help; it is pinned as an acceptance test against Example 11.
- **Inherited parcels** — the beneficiary's side of a deceased-estate transfer is modelled via [Inheritances](#inheritances) (cost base per QC 66053, discount clock per s 115-30). The **estate/LPR side is not**: the executor's own return, assets the executor sells to pay debts (the Maria example's shares), and assets passing to a foreign resident / charity / super fund (CGT in the deceased's date-of-death return) are out of scope — only parcels that pass to the beneficiary are recorded. The **market value at death** for a pre-CGT asset is user-supplied (as valuations are elsewhere), and the pre-21-September-1999 **indexation alternative** is not modelled (the 50% discount is used throughout). **LPR expenditure is only recordable on an AUD inheritance** (2026-08-18): the cost-base pipeline converts a parcel at **one** rate — its (deemed) acquisition month's, which under `DeceasedCostBase` is the *deceased's* acquisition month — while the LPR incurs their expenditure after the death, so on a foreign parcel the fee would be translated at a month that can predate it by decades (a US$1,000 fee incurred at 0.50 reported as A$500 against a 2015 rate of 2.00, where its own month gives A$2,000). Recording an AUD amount correctly would need a second cost-base element the pipeline translates separately, which the single-rate design (see *Cost-base FX timing* above) deliberately does not have, so the pair is **refused at write time** rather than answered wrongly: a foreign inherited parcel's LPR fee has no home, and folding it into `cost_base` by hand would translate it at the same wrong month. Australian LPR fees on Australian holdings — the ordinary case — are unaffected. Two entry conventions follow from the estate side being out of scope, both documented on [Inheritances](#inheritances) and neither checkable from any stored fact: the `cost_base` you enter must have **indexation recalculated out** where the death was on or after 21 September 1999 (QC 66053), and it must be **apportioned to your own share** of the holding where a parcel is split between beneficiaries — the other beneficiaries are not modelled, so a part interest is entered as your units against your share of the deceased's cost base.
- **Crypto assets** — investment crypto is modelled as the exchange-less [`Crypto` listing](#listings) flowing through the ordinary CGT machinery (`docs/ato/crypto-cgt.md`). There is no crypto-specific *operation*: each event below is a CGT event the ordinary trade entry already records, and this is how each is entered. A **crypto-to-crypto swap** is a Sell at the market-value proceeds plus a Buy of the acquired asset at the same value (`docs/ato/crypto-cgt.md`, Katrina). **Wrapping or unwrapping** a token is that same swap — it *is* a CGT event, with capital proceeds equal to the wrapped token's market value (`docs/ato/crypto-wrapping.md`, Kal). A **chain split**'s new asset is neither income nor a gain on receipt and carries a **nil cost base** dated the split: a Buy at a price of `0` (`docs/ato/crypto-chain-splits.md`, Alex); where no post-split asset continues the original, the abandoned original is closed by a [`WorthlessShares`](#corporate-actions) action with `worthless_event: "C2Cancellation"`, whose capital loss is its cost base (Ming). An **initial-allocation airdrop** is the same nil-cost-base Buy (Josh) — *not* an income row, which is the established-token case. **Staking rewards and established-token airdrops** are ordinary income at their receipt-date market value plus a Buy at that value: enter the income as an [income](#income) row with `income_type: "OtherIncome"`, which reports at **item 24, other income** — its own tax-summary line and its own table in the annual tax report, in no dividend total (`docs/ato/crypto-staking-airdrops.md`). What is *not* modelled is any split of that income by source: item 24 is reported as one figure, so a foreign-sourced reward needing question 20 instead is the taxpayer's own reclassification. The **personal-use-asset exemption** is not modelled — every crypto holding is treated as an investment. **Foreign-currency cash balances** (Div 775 forex gains — ordinary income, not CGT) are deferred to a separate specification; that deferral never reaches a crypto holding, because a crypto asset — a stablecoin included — is not 'foreign currency' for Division 775 (`docs/ato/crypto-not-foreign-currency.md`, TD 2014/25 and the 2023 statutory exclusion), so it stays a CGT asset like any other crypto.
- **Intraday prices** — the [closing-price history](#closing-prices) stores one closing/reference price per listing per day; intraday prices are not stored. A back-dated fact does not auto-backfill price history — it only marks the affected [report snapshots](#report-snapshots) stale; the daily jobs self-heal only their bounded windows (14 calendar days, for prices and snapshots alike), so older history is backfilled on demand via `POST /closing_prices/backfill` (then regenerate the affected snapshots via `POST /report_snapshots/generate` or `regenerate_all`).
- **A manually entered price is one-way** (2026-07-28) — a hand-entered [closing price](#closing-prices) can only ever be replaced by another hand-entered one: the scheduled job and backfill skip it as an already-ok day, an explicit re-fetch is refused `422`, and it cannot be deleted (an ok row never is). So a day priced by hand stays that way even if the provider later starts serving it correctly, and a manual price entered against the wrong date cannot be removed — only overwritten with the right figure, unless the listing declares an [`unpriced_before`](#closing-prices) later than that date, the one span where a stored ok price is deletable because valuation reads none of it. That one-way rule is what keeps a valued price from being deleted out from under a stored snapshot. Overwriting loses nothing, though: `closing_prices` is [audited](#row-history), so every superseded price and its provenance stay recoverable.
- **An expense covering more than one financial year is not apportioned for you** (2026-08-17) — one [investment expense](#investment-expenses) row is one `date_incurred`, one financial year, and its whole `amount` deducted in that year. Two ordinary share-investor expenses are spread across years instead (`docs/ato/expense-time-apportionment.md`): **borrowing expenses** over $100 — loan establishment fees, legal expenses, stamp duty on the loan — over **5 years or the loan term, whichever is shorter** (ATO *Dividend income deductions*, QC 104069; s 25-25), and a **prepaid expense** whose eligible service period exceeds 12 months or ends after the last day of the next income year, apportioned **by days** — `amount × (days of the service period in the year ÷ total days)` — over the lesser of the service period and 10 years (*Deductions for prepaid expenses*, QC 106556: $1,250 over 396 days → $572 then $678). A prepayment *inside* the 12-month rule is immediately deductible, which is the case the one-date model gets right by construction. The workaround is **one row per financial year** carrying that year's apportioned share (see [Investment expenses](#investment-expenses)); a multi-year expense keyed as a single row claims the whole deduction in year one and **nothing refuses or flags it**. Not modelled because the honest version is a `service_period_start`/`service_period_end` pair the [tax summary](#tax-summary) splits by days — new columns, a day-count apportionment, extra [annual tax report](#annual-tax-report) rows, and a different answer to "which year is this row in" than every other dated record gives.
- **Statement entry** — the income form's franking selector auto-computes franking credits at the **30% corporate rate** printed on typical statements only; 25% base-rate-entity dividends and partially franked payments are entered via the advanced component fields. Statement figures are keyed in manually — there is no statement parsing/import.
- **Franking: the 30%-at-risk test and the related payments rule** (2026-08-17) — being a **qualified person** for a franking credit takes more than the 45/90-day count the [franking at-risk](#franking-at-risk) walk performs (`docs/ato/you-and-your-shares-dividends.md`). Two further conditions are **not modelled**, and neither has anywhere to be recorded, so no stored fact could test them. (1) The **30%-at-risk test** — you can't count days on which you have 30% or less of the ordinary financial risks of loss and opportunities for gain from owning the shares, e.g. because of hedges, options and futures. A hedge or derivative position over the holding therefore shortens the at-risk period, while the walk counts every day of ownership between the ex-date and the disposal as an at-risk day; hedges, options and futures are not recordable. (2) The **related payments rule** — a holder under an obligation to pass the benefit of the dividend to someone else is not a qualified person for it, and that test applies *separately* from the holding period: the small-shareholder exemption exempts a holder "entitled to franking credits for all shares that satisfy the related payments rule" **without having to satisfy the holding period rule**, so a related payment is not excused by the A$5,000 threshold the way a short holding is. Related payments are not recordable. Consequently the [tax summary](#tax-summary)'s `franking_credits` and the at-risk report's own all-clear both assume the taxpayer holds the shares unhedged and under no related-payment obligation: what they deny is a floor, not necessarily the whole denial. If either applies to a holding, adjust the claim at return time.
- **Foreign tax on a capital gain you realise yourself is not recordable** (2026-08-19) — foreign income tax reaches the [tax summary](#tax-summary)'s `foreign_tax_offsets` line (**20O**) from exactly two places: an [income](#income) row's `foreign_tax_paid` — foreign tax on foreign *income* — and an [AMMA statement](#amma-statements)'s `foreign_tax_credits` plus `foreign_tax_credits_capital_gains`, the latter being a trust distributing a foreign **capital gain**, apportioned here to its assessable part (see [Tax summary](#tax-summary)). There is no third: a [Sell](#sells) carries **no foreign-tax column**, so foreign tax a source country levies on a disposal *you* make has nowhere to be entered and cannot reach the offset at all. That is a scope cut, not an oversight. Under the usual treaty position a source country may tax a non-resident's gain only on **real property** and land-rich interests (`docs/ato/fito-capital-gains-apportionment.md`, QC 104349) — assets this system does not record in the first place, so the disposal such tax attaches to could not be entered either; the AMIT/MIT distribution path above is where a listed-share investor actually meets a foreign-taxed capital gain, and it *is* modelled. **What to do** if you nonetheless pay it — a directly held listing on one of the few exchanges whose country taxes non-residents' share gains: **work that offset out separately and add it to 20O yourself**, apportioning it to the assessable part of the gain by the same Division 115 rule this report applies to the AMMA path (halve it for a discounted gain), then reconcile against the figure reported here. **Do not fake it as an income row** — a `foreign_tax_paid` amount there is a component of that distribution's cash, so the row would report a distribution that never happened in the [income](#income) list, the [listing activity ledger](#listing-activity) and the [annual tax report](#annual-tax-report)'s per-record income detail, and its foreign tax would join 20O **unapportioned**, over-claiming a discounted gain by exactly the half the AMMA path reduces away.
- **Gifts / off-market related-party transfers** (2026-06-10) — a gift of shares or crypto (or any nil-consideration / non-arm's-length transfer) is a **CGT disposal at market value** under the market-value substitution rule (`docs/ato/capital-proceeds-market-value-substitution.md`): the giver's capital proceeds are the asset's market value at the time of the gift, and the recipient's first-element cost base is likewise that market value. There is no dedicated gift entry path — enter a gift out as a manual Sell at market-value proceeds, and a gift in as a manual Buy at market-value cost.
- **Pre-CGT holdings** (2026-06-10; enforced at write time 2026-07-13) — a parcel acquired before **20 September 1985** is outside CGT entirely (gains and losses on it are disregarded); pre-CGT holdings are not modelled, and they **cannot be entered**: a [trade](#trades) or [Sell](#sells) dated before 20 September 1985, and an [inheritance](#inheritances) whose date of death pre-dates it (the parcel would be pre-CGT in the beneficiary's own hands under s 115-30), are rejected with `422` — so the system can no longer be handed a parcel it would wrongly compute a capital gain or loss on. (The one modelled pre-CGT interaction is an inherited parcel that was pre-CGT *in the deceased's hands*: [Inheritances](#inheritances) applies the market-value-at-death cost base, and the parcel is post-CGT in the beneficiary's hands.)
- **Indexation method** (2026-06-10) — for an asset acquired before **21 September 1999** an individual may index the cost base for inflation (frozen at the 30 September 1999 CPI) *instead of* applying the 50% discount (`docs/ato/indexing-the-cost-base.md`). The discount almost always gives an individual the better result, so indexation is not modelled — the 50% discount is used throughout (the inherited-parcels entry above states the same for the deceased-estate case).
- **Collectables and personal-use assets — quarantined losses** (2026-07-29) — a **collectable** (jewellery, art, antiques, coins) and a **personal-use asset** (a boat, furniture) are separate CGT asset classes with their own loss rules: a capital loss from a collectable can *only* reduce a capital gain from another collectable — never a gain on shares — and is carried forward in its own pool until one arises; a loss from a personal-use asset is **disregarded entirely** (`docs/ato/capital-gains-question-18.md`, the Kathleen examples, where a $500 jewellery loss leaves the share gains untouched and lands at label V on its own). Neither class is modelled: there is no asset-class dimension on a [listing](#listings) and one single loss pool in the [net capital gain](#net-capital-gain) report, so a collectable entered as an ordinary listing would have its loss **wrongly offset share gains** and understate the net capital gain.
